Perfect. I've read all four files. Here's the detailed analysis of what you asked for:

---

## **Report JSON Format**

Both NVDA and META reports follow the same structure. The JSON contains:

**Top-level fields:**
- `ticker` — symbol (e.g., "NVDA")
- `company` — full name (e.g., "NVIDIA Corporation")
- `date` — analysis date (YYYY-MM-DD format)
- `stages` — object containing skill outputs
- `meta` — summary metadata (profit_stage, track, ai_layer, confidence)

**stages.signal fields** (from Signal skill):
- `verdict` — BUY | WATCH | CAUTION
- `profit_stage` — ESTABLISHED | EMERGING
- `track` — GROWTH | YIELD
- `ai_layer` — INFRASTRUCTURE | APPLICATION | MODEL | INCUMBENT | N/A
- `clean_eps_ttm` — SBC-stripped TTM EPS in dollars
- `sbc_stripped` — boolean; always true post-ABA-110
- `sbc_adjustment_per_share` — dollar difference (reported vs. clean)
- `peg_ratio` — P/E ÷ 5-year EPS CAGR (x.xx)
- `ps_ratio` — price-to-sales (x.xx)
- `rule_of_40` — null (not yet implemented)
- `qualitative` — PASS | FLAG | FAIL
- `qualitative_note` — reason string
- `signal` — BUY | WATCH | CAUTION (final verdict)
- `model_ready` — YES | CONDITIONAL | NO
- `condition` — null or explanation if CONDITIONAL

**stages.model fields** (from Model skill — two-stage DCF):
- `method` — "two-stage DCF"
- `profit_stage` — ESTABLISHED | EMERGING
- `current_price` — market price (float)
- `shares_diluted` — diluted share count (integer)
- `net_debt` — net debt in dollars (can be negative if net cash)
- `fcf_ttm` — trailing-twelve-month free cash flow (SBC-stripped)
- `fcf_ttm_reported` — reported FCF (for audit trail)
- `sbc_ttm` — stock-based compensation TTM
- `fcf_margin_ttm` — clean FCF / revenue
- `fcf_margin_ttm_reported` — reported FCF / revenue
- `historical_fcf_margins` — array of 4-5 years with year and both margin types
- `fcf_cagr_3y` — 3-year FCF compound annual growth rate
- `growth_rate` — object containing:
  - `trailing_3y_cagr` — calculated 3Y FCF CAGR
  - `consensus_5y_growth` — null or analyst consensus 5Y EPS growth
  - `fallback_ceiling` — generic 18% cap (ABA-111)
  - `applied_base_cagr` — the actual Y2-Y5 growth rate used in model
  - `cap_applied` — boolean
  - `cap_source` — "playbook" or "generic" or "user"
- `ntm_revenue` — next-twelve-months revenue (consensus)
- `base_wacc` — weighted-average cost of capital for base case
- `base_wacc_source` — "playbook" or "generic" or "user"

**scenarios** (bear / base / bull):
- `y1_fcf` — first-year FCF (usually TTM or mildly adjusted)
- `y2_5_cagr` — year 2-5 growth rate (decimal)
- `terminal_growth` — perpetual growth rate (2.5% base, varies by scenario)
- `wacc` — scenario-specific WACC (base ± 100 bps typically)
- `intrinsic_value_per_share` — DCF output (dollars per share)
- `upside_pct` — (IV - price) / price (can be negative for bear)
- `narrative` — thesis explanation for the scenario

**intrinsic_value_range:**
- `bear`, `base`, `bull` — three IVs in dollars

**Valuation assessment:**
- `range_vs_price` — e.g., "WITHIN BEAR-BASE", "WITHIN BASE-BULL", "ABOVE BULL", "BELOW BEAR"
- `sensitivity.dominant_driver` — typically "WACC"
- `sensitivity.note` — e.g., "±100 bps WACC moves base IV by ±17%"
- `sensitivity_table.wacc_axis` — [0.08, 0.085, 0.09, 0.095, 0.10]
- `sensitivity_table.terminal_growth_axis` — [0.015, 0.02, 0.025, 0.03, 0.035]
- `sensitivity_table.intrinsic_value_per_share` — 5×5 grid of IVs
- `sensitivity_table.base_cell` — {"row": 2, "col": 2} (marks where base case sits in grid)

**Position sizing:**
- `band` — e.g., "3–4% of portfolio" or "2–3% of portfolio"
- `lower_pct`, `upper_pct` — bounds as integers
- `signal_verdict` — the signal's verdict (used to size the band)
- `range_vs_price` — as above
- `margin_of_safety_pct` — upside_pct of base case (59.77 for NVDA, 32.6 for META)
- `confidence_cap_applied` — boolean (true if playbook_confidence capped the band)
- `rationale` — English explanation of the band logic

**Playbook section** (ABA-112):
- `loaded` — boolean
- `path` — e.g., "playbooks/NVDA.md"
- `last_updated` — YYYY-MM-DD
- `overrides_applied` — array of field names that were overridden (base_wacc, growth_ceiling, bear_narrative, base_narrative, bull_narrative, failure_modes, confidence_anchor, terminal_margin, etc.)
- `failure_modes` — array of strings describing downside scenarios and what triggers them

---

## **DCF Output Fields Summary**

The core DCF output is the **scenarios** array with three entries (bear, base, bull), each producing:
1. **y1_fcf** — starting cash flow
2. **y2_5_cagr** — 4-year growth assumption
3. **terminal_growth** — perpetuity growth
4. **wacc** — discount rate for that scenario
5. **intrinsic_value_per_share** — the IV itself (dollars)
6. **upside_pct** — (IV − price) / price as percentage
7. **narrative** — thesis for that scenario

The `intrinsic_value_range` object then summarizes all three IVs as { bear, base, bull }.

The `sensitivity_table` provides a 5×5 grid showing how IV changes across WACC (y-axis) and terminal growth (x-axis), allowing readers to understand the key drivers.

---

## **Roadmap "Now" Section**

The **Now** section (lines 101–150) lists six work items currently in flight:

1. **ABA-110 + ABA-111** — DONE 2026-05-17 (SBC stripping and growth-rate ceiling)
2. **ABA-104** — Base-year FCF growth-rate spike (spike task, ~2 days appetite)
3. **ABA-112** — Playbook loader for watchlist tickers (~1 week, cap)
4. **Write playbook: ASML** — (2–3 days after ABA-112 lands)
5. **Write playbook: GOOG** — (2–3 days after ABA-112 lands)
6. **`/stock-explain` skill** — Plain-English walkthrough of model output (~1 week, separate skill)
7. **`/stock-portfolio` skill** — Portfolio-level table of all seven tickers (shipped 2026-05-17, pending QA; reads from `reports/`)
8. **ABA-115** — Glidepath and scenario fan visuals (~1 week)

Key success criteria for this cycle (lines 20–26):
- Model can be trusted (ABA-104 resolved or clearly documented)
- Analysis tailored to at least two stocks via playbooks
- Portfolio comparisons work in one invocation
- Valuations understandable to non-experts
- Visual scenarios and trajectory views in UI

---

## **DESIGN.md Skill Architecture Section**

The **Architecture Overview** (lines 9–21) shows five skills with a router:

```
/stock-equity    → Router        orchestrates full chain or dispatches to sub-skills
/stock-screen    → Screen        fast go/no-go; two variants by profit stage
/stock-signal    → Signal        GARP signal analysis; outputs MODEL_READY flag
/stock-model     → Model         conviction-building model; two variants by profit stage
/stock-timing    → Timing        when-to-act overlay; standalone invocation
```

**Key principles:**
1. All skills use the `stock-` prefix (e.g., `/stock-model`, not `/model`) to avoid collision with built-ins
2. Router infers **profit stage** (ESTABLISHED vs. EMERGING) and **track** (GROWTH vs. YIELD) before dispatch
3. Signal outputs a structured block with a `MODEL_READY` flag that Model checks before proceeding
4. Model has two variants: **two-stage DCF for ESTABLISHED** (5Y FCF + Gordon terminal) and **revenue-multiple exit for EMERGING** (inflection + dilution schedule)
5. Every skill writes a report JSON to `reports/TICKER_YYYYMMDD.json`
6. Playbook loader (ABA-112) reads `playbooks/TICKER.md` to apply ticker-specific overrides (WACC, growth ceiling, failure modes, narratives)

The **Skill Authoring Conventions** (lines 81–100) mandate:
- SBC stripping is step zero
- AI layer classification (INFRASTRUCTURE / APPLICATION / MODEL / INCUMBENT / N/A) is applied in qualitative filters
- Every skill web-searches for inputs before asking the user
- Skills state what they found and what they assumed

---

## **About `/stock-explain`**

In the roadmap, `/stock-explain` appears in the **Now** section (lines 133–138) as a separate skill:

> **Problem:** For the operator, we believe model outputs use sophisticated terminology that's correct but not understandable on a quick read — making the IV a "stupid number" rather than something usable. Existing skills' methodology sections are present but dense.
>
> **Success criterion:** New `/stock-explain TICKER` skill takes the latest report for a ticker and produces a plain-English walkthrough — what the number means, how it was derived, what assumptions are doing the work, what would change it. Tested on two reports; operator confirms it reads as friendly without losing the audit trail.
>
> **Theme:** differentiator (legibility)

It's a **separate skill** (not an inline output section), designed to read the cached report JSON and produce a friendly walkthrough for non-experts. Expected appetite: ~1 week.

---

That covers all four files. The report format is stable; DCF fields are clearly defined; the roadmap shows `/stock-explain` as a legibility differentiator in the Now phase; and the skill architecture is built on playbooks for coverage depth and a five-skill chain with a router orchestrator.

I've read all three skill definition files. Here's a comprehensive summary of the exact structure, patterns, and conventions across these skills:

---

## **FILE STRUCTURE & FRONTMATTER (Common Pattern)**

All three skills use identical YAML frontmatter:
```yaml
---
name: [skill-name]
description: [one-paragraph description of invocation and purpose]
---
```

The description appears in both the frontmatter and as a live tooltip in the system-reminder at the top of the session. Each includes:
- **Command syntax** with optional flags
- **Purpose statement** (one sentence)
- **Use-case triggers** (when to invoke this skill)

---

## **SECTION STRUCTURE (All Skills Follow This Pattern)**

1. **Identity** — skill name, command, purpose
2. **Methodology** — theoretical foundation (GARP, DCF, portfolio view)
3. **Input Schema** — table of inputs, sources, fallback behavior
4. **Execution Phases** — the operational steps (GATHER, VALIDATE, COMPUTE, THRESHOLD, OUTPUT)
5. **Output Schema** — exact format of deliverables (text block + JSON)
6. **Data Fetching Behaviour** — MCP server contracts
7. **Invocation Patterns** — example command lines
8. **Dependencies** — required tools/files
9. **Tech-Specific Rules** — domain constraints (e.g., SBC stripping in stock-signal/model)
10. **Override Rules** — conditional logic and precedence
11. **Common Rationalisations** — pre-butted objections (why each rule exists)

---

## **REPORT FILE NAMING & PATHS**

**Convention:** `reports/TICKER_YYYYMMDD.json` where `YYYYMMDD` is today's date.

- **Read behavior:** Look for same-day file first; reject older files (ABA-30 smoke check: `/stock-signal TICKER first` phrase is verified)
- **Write behavior:** Always write to `reports/` (create via `mkdir -p` if absent); read-modify-write if file exists (preserve other stages)
- **Merge rule:** Each skill writes its own `stages.{signal|model}` block; do **not** overwrite `stages.screen` or other stages
- **Portfolio aggregator:** Reads *all* three stage blocks from the latest per-ticker file; sorts by date descending

---

## **SHARED JSON STRUCTURE (Nested under `stages`)**

```json
{
  "ticker": "TICKER",
  "company": "Company Name",
  "date": "YYYY-MM-DD",
  "stages": {
    "signal": { /* 14 fields, see below */ },
    "model": { /* 25+ fields, see below */ },
    "screen": { /* not detailed in these three files */ }
  },
  "meta": {
    "profit_stage": "[ESTABLISHED | EMERGING]",
    "track": "[GROWTH | YIELD]",
    "ai_layer": "[INFRASTRUCTURE | APPLICATION | MODEL | INCUMBENT | N/A | null]",
    "confidence": "[HIGH | MEDIUM | LOW]"
  }
}
```

---

## **STOCK-SIGNAL: SIGNAL OUTPUT BLOCK (14 JSON fields)**

```
SIGNAL OUTPUT
  Ticker:          [TICKER]
  Company:         [Name or ticker]
  Profit stage:    [ESTABLISHED | EMERGING]
  Track:           [GROWTH | YIELD]
  AI layer:        [enum] — [one-line rationale]

  Clean EPS (TTM): [$X.XX | N/A — reason]
  SBC stripped:    [YES/NO/N/A]
  SBC adjustment:  [per-share $ or N/A]
  PEG ratio:       [number | N/A — reason]
  P/S ratio:       [number]
  Rule of 40:      [N/A — not implemented]

  Qualitative:     [PASS | FLAG | FAIL] — [one-line reason]
  TAM/Optionality: [note — INFRASTRUCTURE and INCUMBENT only]

  Signal:          [BUY | WATCH | CAUTION]
  MODEL_READY:     [YES | CONDITIONAL | NO]
  Condition:       [if CONDITIONAL — the user must confirm]
```

**JSON fields (14 total):**
1. `verdict` (= `signal`)
2. `profit_stage`
3. `track`
4. `ai_layer`
5. `clean_eps_ttm`
6. `sbc_stripped` (boolean, not string)
7. `sbc_adjustment_per_share`
8. `peg_ratio`
9. `ps_ratio`
10. `rule_of_40`
11. `qualitative`
12. `qualitative_note`
13. `signal` (= `verdict`)
14. `model_ready` + `condition` (if CONDITIONAL)

**Key convention:** All 14 fields must appear **every run** in the text block. No field is omitted. Unavailable fields use placeholder text (e.g., `N/A — reason`). JSON uses `null`, not empty strings or 0.

---

## **STOCK-MODEL: MODEL OUTPUT BLOCK + SCENARIOS**

The text OUTPUT block is extensive. **Structure:**

```
MODEL OUTPUT
  Ticker:        [TICKER]
  Method:        [Two-stage DCF | Pre-profit variant]
  Profit stage:  [ESTABLISHED | EMERGING]
  Upstream:      [source, verdict, MODEL_READY]
  Playbook:      [Loaded: ... | Not loaded — generic logic]

  Current price: $X.XX
  Shares (dil.): XXX.X M
  Net debt:      $X.X B
  FCF (TTM):     [reported | clean | SBC | margin clean | margin reported | CAGR]
  Historical clean FCF margin: [per-fiscal-year series]
  NTM revenue:   $X.X B
  Base WACC:     X.X% (source)

  [Three scenario blocks, each with:]
  Bear/Base/Bull: $X.XX / share — [N]% upside/downside
    Y1 FCF: [calculation]  |  Y2-5 CAGR: [value + cap note if applicable]  |  g: [terminal growth]  |  WACC: [rate]
    Narrative: [one-liner]
    [Engagement modifier details if --engagement-modifier flag and status=applied]

  Range vs price: [MARGIN OF SAFETY | WITHIN BEAR–BASE | WITHIN BASE–BULL | PRICE EXCEEDS RANGE]
  Sensitivity:    [dominant driver one-liner]
  Sensitivity grid: [5×5 WACC × terminal-growth table, base cell marked with *]

  Position sizing: [L–U% of portfolio]
    Signal: [verdict] × Range: [...] (MoS [+/-]X.X%)
    Rationale: [one-line with all three drivers]

  Monitor: [failure_modes_1; failure_mode_2; ...]  ← playbook only
```

**Key JSON structure (25+ fields):**
- `method`, `profit_stage`, `current_price`, `shares_diluted`, `net_debt`
- `fcf_ttm`, `fcf_ttm_reported`, `sbc_ttm`, `fcf_margin_ttm`, `fcf_margin_ttm_reported`
- `fcf_cagr_3y`
- `historical_fcf_margins` — per-fiscal-year array (newest-first) with clean and reported margins
- `growth_rate` — object with trailing, consensus, fallback, applied, cap_applied, cap_source
- `ntm_revenue`, `base_wacc`, `base_wacc_source`
- `scenarios` — object with bear/base/bull, each containing:
  - `y1_fcf`, `y2_5_cagr`, `terminal_growth`, `wacc`
  - `intrinsic_value_per_share`, `upside_pct`
  - `narrative`
- `intrinsic_value_range` — {bear, base, bull} as flat convenience field
- `range_vs_price`, `sensitivity`, `sensitivity_table`
- `position_sizing` — {band, lower_pct, upper_pct, signal_verdict, range_vs_price, margin_of_safety_pct, confidence_cap_applied, rationale}
- `playbook` — {loaded, path, last_updated, overrides_applied, failure_modes}
- `engagement_modifier` (optional, only when `--engagement-modifier` flag present)

**SBC stripping is mandatory:** `/stock-signal` Step 5 strips SBC from EPS; `/stock-model` Step 1 strips SBC from FCF. Both use the **same** SBC figure for consistency (ABA-110). The clean (SBC-stripped) values anchor the DCF.

---

## **STOCK-PORTFOLIO: READ-ONLY AGGREGATOR**

**Input:** Latest reports for each of 7 canonical tickers (`COVERAGE.md`)
**Output:** Ranked table + JSON snapshot

**Table columns:**
- Ticker | Current price | Base IV | MoS % | Verdict | Last updated

**Verdict labels (inherited from `/stock-model`):**
- `MARGIN OF SAFETY`
- `WITHIN BEAR-BASE`
- `WITHIN BASE-BULL`
- `PRICE EXCEEDS RANGE`
- `NO_MODEL` (new to portfolio skill)
- `STALE_MODEL` (new to portfolio skill; model > 14 days old)

**Price resolution order (fallback chain):**
1. Signal (most recent)
2. Screen
3. Model (oldest, since snapshot taken at model-run time)

**MoS formula (inherited from `/stock-model` POSITION SIZING):**
```
MoS % = (base_iv / current_price − 1) × 100
```

**JSON snapshot schema:** 7-ticker array with per-ticker fields (current_price, iv_base, mos_pct, range_vs_price, sources map, gaps array).

---

## **SHARED PATTERNS & CONVENTIONS**

### **1. Model gating (stock-model → stock-signal contract)**

`/stock-model` reads `MODEL_READY` from upstream Signal:
- `YES` → proceed to DCF
- `CONDITIONAL` → halt and ask user for `--confirm` flag
- `NO` → refuse; point back to `/stock-signal`

The `Condition` string from Signal is surfaced verbatim; no paraphrasing.

### **2. Playbook loading (stock-model only)**

`playbooks/TICKER.md` provides ticker-specific overrides:
- `base_wacc`, `growth_ceiling`, `terminal_margin`
- `bear_narrative`, `base_narrative`, `bull_narrative`
- `scenario_axes` (advanced per-scenario overrides)
- `failure_modes` (surfaced in OUTPUT)
- `confidence_anchor` (caps confidence at stated level)

Priority: `--wacc=X.X` flag (runtime) > `base_wacc` in playbook > MIP (user paste). Playbook loading is optional; absence is noted (`No playbook for TICKER — using generic logic`) and does not fail the run.

### **3. Manual Input Protocol (MIP)**

Fires when critical data is null or missing:
- Always grouped in a single paste-in prompt (never round-trip)
- Accepts user paste with format: `field=value` per field
- Tagged as `source: "user_paste"` and caps `meta.confidence` at MEDIUM
- For SBC paste-in on stock-model: `source: "user_paste"` caps confidence at MEDIUM, unconditionally

### **4. Confidence levels**

- `HIGH`: All required data from MCP (no pastes), scenario range passed first-time, no manual inputs except WACC confirmation.
- `MEDIUM`: 2–3y FCF history, or any paste-in (net_debt, shares, SBC, WACC), or range integrity required user re-input.
- `LOW`: <2y FCF history, or WACC constraint violated and mid-scenario override needed.

**Confidence cap rules:** Take the min of all prior stages and the current stage — never upgrade. SBC paste-in on stock-model is unconditionally one-way cap to MEDIUM.

### **5. SBC stripping (mandatory in both signal and model)**

Both skills strip SBC from earnings/FCF:
- **stock-signal Step 5:** `Clean EPS (TTM) = reported EPS − (SBC_TTM / shares_outstanding)`
- **stock-model Step 1:** `fcf_ttm = fcf_ttm_reported − sbc_ttm`

The SBC source is always `get_financials(ticker)` → `Stock Based Compensation` (cashflow row). When null, both skills fire the MIP and ask the user to paste the TTM SBC in dollars. Never silently assume zero.

### **6. AI layer classification (stock-signal Step 2)**

Pure qualitative reasoning; no MCP call. Decision tree (use first match):
1. Revenue from AI chips/cloud/datacenter hardware → `INFRASTRUCTURE`
2. AI embedded in SaaS as core differentiator, subscription → `APPLICATION`
3. Primary product is foundation model or model API → `MODEL`
4. Large profitable tech company using AI as optionality/defence → `INCUMBENT`
5. AI not material → `N/A`

Always output a one-line rationale. Examples in SKILL.md stock-signal section.

### **7. TAM penetration check (INFRASTRUCTURE only)**

Fires for INFRASTRUCTURE companies (stock-signal Step 3):
- Penetration > 50%: flag as TAM ceiling risk → Qualitative = FLAG (or FAIL if primary signal = CAUTION)
- Penetration 30–50%: neutral note
- Penetration < 30%: positive note ("runway remains")

Always state assumption: "Data centre GPU TAM: ~$250B est.; penetration = X%."

### **8. AI optionality premium (INCUMBENT only)**

Fires for INCUMBENT companies (stock-signal Step 4):
- Estimate AI-attributed % of TTM capex
- Apply 5× revenue multiple on AI capex (conservative proxy)
- AI optionality premium % = (AI capex × 5) / market_cap × 100
- Premium > 20%: flag as unverified optionality; Qualitative = FLAG
- Premium ≤ 20%: note that investment appears proportionate

**The percentage must always be a number, never N/A.** State all assumptions explicitly.

### **9. FCF CAGR capping (stock-model Step 3, ABA-111)**

Trailing 3y CAGR is useful floor but poor central case (trough-extrapolation risk). Cap against:
1. `get_estimates.eps_growth_5y` (consensus) — **highest priority**
2. Playbook `growth_ceiling` (if present)
3. Fallback: 18% (ESTABLISHED) or 35% (pre-profit)

Formula: `base_cagr = min(fcf_cagr_3y, ceiling)`. The cap only narrows, never raises. Always output the cap source and raw trailing CAGR when the cap fires:
- `Y2-5 CAGR: X.X% (capped from trailing Y.Y% by <consensus | playbook | fallback_ceiling>)`

Bear and bull scenarios scale off the capped `base_cagr`, not raw trailing CAGR.

### **10. Engagement modifier (stock-model, opt-in with `--engagement-modifier` flag)**

**Advanced feature (FR1–FR8, NFR4–NFR5):**
- Reads KPI from `skills/_shared/engagement_kpi_map.json`
- Fetches latest 8-K Ex 99.1 (EDGAR MCP or direct HTTP)
- Extracts KPI value and YoY change
- Applies guardrail: checks 30-day EPS revision direction (Yahoo `/analysis/` page)
- Only applies modifier if engagement direction **agrees** with revision direction
- Caps modifier impact on base IV at ≤5% (Step 6b bisection)
- Caps `meta.confidence` at MEDIUM
- Records full audit trail (kpi_map_schema_version, source URL, revision direction, agreement flag)

When flag absent: entire block omitted from JSON. When flag present but modifier not applied: `status ∈ {unavailable, no_kpi_mapping, user_skipped, direction_disagreement}`, all other fields null.

---

## **THRESHOLD RULES & SIGNAL VERDICTS**

### **stock-signal verdicts (profile-keyed, ABA-132 v1.10)**

| Profile | BUY | WATCH | CAUTION |
|---------|-----|-------|---------|
| INFRASTRUCTURE | PEG ≤ 2.0 | PEG ≤ 4.0 | > 4.0 |
| APPLICATION | PEG ≤ 1.5 | PEG ≤ 3.0 | > 3.0 |
| INCUMBENT | PEG ≤ 1.5 | PEG ≤ 4.0 | > 4.0 |
| MODEL | PEG ≤ 1.5 | PEG ≤ 2.5 | > 2.5 |
| N/A | PEG ≤ 1.0 | PEG ≤ 2.5 | > 2.5 |
| Unknown | PEG ≤ 1.0 | PEG ≤ 2.0 | > 2.0 |

**EMERGING (pre-profit) — P/S only (always CAUTION, overridden):**
| BUY | WATCH | CAUTION |
| P/S ≤ 8 | P/S ≤ 20 | > 20 |

**Overrides (applied in order):**
1. Override 1 — EMERGING always → Signal = CAUTION, MODEL_READY = NO (pre-profit base effect)
2. Override 2 — Transition year (1st or 2nd profitable year) → MODEL_READY = CONDITIONAL
3. Override 2.5 — Clean EPS negative because SBC, but GAAP-profitable + FCF-positive → Signal = min(threshold, WATCH), MODEL_READY = CONDITIONAL, Qualitative = FLAG (route to pre-profit `/stock-model` variant)
4. Override 3 — Qualitative = FAIL → Signal = CAUTION, MODEL_READY = NO

**MODEL_READY final logic (precedence: NO > CONDITIONAL > YES):**
- `YES`: Signal ∈ {BUY, WATCH}, Qualitative ≠ FAIL, no transition-year, no Override 2.5, ESTABLISHED profit_stage
- `CONDITIONAL`: Signal = WATCH, (transition-year flag set OR Override 2.5 fired OR stub fields pending)
- `NO`: Signal = CAUTION OR Qualitative = FAIL OR profit_stage = EMERGING

---

## **TERMINOLOGY & NAMING**

- **ESTABLISHED:** Profit stage; positive TTM P/E
- **EMERGING:** Profit stage; null/negative/zero TTM P/E; pre-profit
- **Clean (SBC-stripped):** EPS and FCF after stock-based compensation deducted per share
- **Reported (GAAP):** EPS and FCF as published by the company (includes SBC as non-cash, thus treated as "free")
- **TTM:** Trailing twelve months
- **NTM:** Next twelve months (forward consensus estimate)
- **MoS (margin of safety):** `(base_iv / current_price − 1) × 100`
- **FCF margin:** Free cash flow as % of revenue
- **Range vs price:** Label indicating where current price sits in bear–base–bull range (inherited to portfolio)

---

## **KEY DESIGN DECISIONS**

1. **No field omission:** Every field in the text OUTPUT block must appear every run. Missing data uses placeholder text (e.g., `N/A — reason`), never blank or omitted.

2. **JSON normalization:** Use JSON `null`, not empty strings, 0, or "N/A".

3. **Boolean precision:** `sbc_stripped` is `true`/`false`/`null`, never the string `"YES"`.

4. **Date format:** ISO `YYYY-MM-DD` throughout.

5. **SBC is load-bearing:** Both signal and model depend on the same TTM SBC for internal consistency (ABA-110). When null, both fire MIP — never skip or assume.

6. **Read-modify-write:** If `reports/TICKER_YYYYMMDD.json` exists, read it first, then update only the relevant `stages.*` block(s). Do not overwrite the entire file.

7. **Staleness contract:** `/stock-model` rejects `MODEL_READY = YES` when the upstream Signal is older than today's date (same-day date check, ABA-30).

8. **Precedence:** Runtime flag > Playbook > Generic defaults. User override beats everything except `confidence_anchor` (playbook can cap confidence one-way).

---

This is the complete, canonical structure across all three skills. Each one follows the same execution-phase template (GATHER → VALIDATE → COMPUTE → THRESHOLD → OUTPUT), and they share common inputs (SBC, AI layer, MoS formula, JSON nesting, playbook loading, confidence rules). The stock-model gates on stock-signal's MODEL_READY flag to enforce the upstream contract.
```

## Approved Plan (edited by user):
# Plan: ABA-118 — `/stock-explain` skill

## Context

Operators find model outputs correct but opaque — the intrinsic value (IV) is a "stupid number" rather than an actionable signal. ABA-118 creates a new `/stock-explain TICKER` skill that reads the existing cached report JSON and produces a plain-English walkthrough: what the IV means, how it was derived, which assumptions drive it, and what would change it. It is read-only (no writes to `reports/`) and does not modify any existing skill output.

## Breakdown recommendation

**No sub-task breakdown needed.** The deliverable is a single file: `skills/stock-explain/SKILL.md`. The `skill-creator:skill-creator` skill handles the full lifecycle (spec → write → eval). Complexity is in the content design, not engineering. One session is sufficient.

---

## Implementation

### File to create
`skills/stock-explain/SKILL.md`

### Skill to invoke
`skill-creator:skill-creator` — per `AGENTS.md`, all `/stock-*` skills use this tool.

### Skill spec (input to skill-creator)

**Command:** `/stock-explain TICKER`

**Purpose:** Read-only post-processor. Takes the latest `reports/TICKER_YYYYMMDD.json` for a ticker and emits a plain-English narrative walkthrough. No MCP calls. No report writes.

**Inputs (all from report JSON):**
- `stages.signal`: verdict, clean_eps_ttm, peg_ratio, model_ready, qualitative_note, ai_layer, profit_stage
- `stages.model`: current_price, scenarios (bear/base/bull), fcf_ttm, fcf_margin_ttm, growth_rate, base_wacc, net_debt, sensitivity, playbook (failure_modes)
- `meta`: confidence

**Output format — five plain-English sections (no JSON output, no table)**:

**Core writing rule:** Every section must be readable by someone who has never studied finance. Every abbreviation or acronym must be spelled out on first use with a one-phrase plain-English definition (e.g., "free cash flow (the money the business generates after paying to keep itself running)"). No term may appear unexplained. The test: would a smart non-investor understand every sentence without having to look anything up?

1. **The verdict in one sentence** — e.g., "At $130, NVIDIA looks undervalued by about 60% against our base estimate of $207."
2. **How we got there** — walk through the model inputs in conversational terms: "We started with NVIDIA's cleaned-up free cash flow (the money it actually generates after paying employees in stock) of $X billion. We then asked: if that cash flow grows at roughly X% per year for the next five years, and we discount those future dollars back to today's value, what is the business worth right now? That calculation gave us the $207 number."
3. **The assumptions doing the heavy lifting** — name the 2–3 inputs that most move the intrinsic value (typically the growth rate and the discount rate), and show the impact in dollar terms: "If NVIDIA's free cash flow grows at 25% a year instead of 20%, the estimated value per share rises from $207 to $240. The discount rate — roughly 'how much return investors demand to own this stock' — is the other big lever: drop it by 1 percentage point and the value climbs by about $35."
4. **What could break it** — surface playbook `failure_modes` if present; otherwise synthesise 2–3 bear triggers from bear scenario narrative + sensitivity dominant driver. Write each failure mode as a plain sentence: "If demand for AI chips plateaus in 2026, growth could slow to single digits — and our model would be too optimistic."
5. **How confident we are** — translate `meta.confidence` (HIGH/MEDIUM/LOW) into plain language and explain the reason: "We have four years of cash-flow history, a detailed set of company-specific assumptions, and didn't have to make up any missing numbers ourselves — so we'd call this a high-confidence estimate."

**Workflow:**
1. Locate latest report: `reports/TICKER_*.json` sorted by date descending; fail fast if none found (message: "No report found for TICKER — run `/stock-signal TICKER` and `/stock-model TICKER` first.")
2. Check both `stages.signal` and `stages.model` are present; if either missing, name which to run.
3. Generate the five sections above from the JSON fields — no MCP calls, no live data fetch.
4. Emit output to terminal only. No file writes.

**Common rationalisations to pre-rebut in the skill:**
- "I'll fetch live data to make it more current" — No. Explain uses the cached report only; freshness = date in report.
- "I'll add technical terms for completeness" — No. If a term would need a parenthetical to be understood, use only the parenthetical.
- "I'll emit a JSON block for auditability" — No. This skill is legibility-only; audit trail is in the report JSON itself.

### Additional tasks in the same session

1. **Mark ABA-118 In Progress** in Linear (`mcp__linear-server__save_issue`) — first action before writing any files.
2. **Update `docs/roadmap.md`** — the `/stock-explain` entry in Now already exists; add a note that it's In Progress.
3. **Test on two reports** — run `/stock-explain NVDA` and `/stock-explain META` against the existing reports and confirm output reads as friendly.
4. **Commit** with message `feat(skill): add /stock-explain plain-English walkthrough (ABA-118)`.
5. **Mark ABA-118 Done** in Linear after commit is pushed.

---

## Verification

1. `skills/stock-explain/SKILL.md` exists and follows standard skill frontmatter + section structure.
2. Running `/stock-explain NVDA` against `reports/NVDA_20260520.json` produces a five-section narrative with no technical jargon left unexplained.
3. Running `/stock-explain META` works similarly.
4. Running `/stock-explain FAKE` (no report) emits the "no report found" error, not a crash.
5. No files in `reports/` were modified.

```

{
  "skill": "skill-creator:skill-creator",
  "args": "Create a new skill: /stock-explain TICKER\n\n## Purpose\nRead-only post-processor. Takes the latest `reports/TICKER_YYYYMMDD.json` for a ticker and emits a five-section plain-English narrative walkthrough of the model output. No MCP calls. No report writes. Output to terminal only.\n\n## File to create\n`skills/stock-explain/SKILL.md`\n\n## Existing skill conventions to follow\n- Frontmatter: `name`, `description` (one paragraph covering command syntax, purpose, use-case triggers)\n- Standard sections: Identity, Methodology, Input Schema, Execution Phases, Output Schema, Invocation Patterns, Dependencies, Common Rationalisations\n- Skill description in system-reminder should be self-contained\n\n## Input schema (all from report JSON — no MCP calls)\nFrom `stages.signal`: verdict, clean_eps_ttm, peg_ratio, model_ready, qualitative_note, ai_layer, profit_stage\nFrom `stages.model`: current_price, scenarios (bear/base/bull with intrinsic_value_per_share, upside_pct, narrative, y2_5_cagr, wacc, terminal_growth), fcf_ttm, fcf_margin_ttm, growth_rate (applied_base_cagr, cap_applied, cap_source), base_wacc, base_wacc_source, net_debt, sensitivity (dominant_driver, note), playbook (loaded, failure_modes)\nFrom `meta`: confidence\n\n## Execution phases\n1. **LOCATE** — Find `reports/TICKER_*.json` sorted by date descending; pick the most recent. If none: emit \"No report found for TICKER — run `/stock-signal TICKER` and `/stock-model TICKER` first.\" and stop.\n2. **VALIDATE** — Check `stages.signal` and `stages.model` both exist. If either is missing, name which skill to run first and stop.\n3. **EXPLAIN** — Generate the five sections below from JSON fields. No calculations beyond what's already in the report. No MCP calls.\n4. **EMIT** — Print sections to terminal. No file writes.\n\n## Output format — five plain-English sections (no JSON block, no markdown table)\n\n**Core writing rule (non-negotiable):** Every section must be readable by someone who has never studied finance. Every abbreviation or acronym MUST be spelled out on first use with a brief plain-English definition in parentheses. The test: a smart non-investor should understand every sentence without looking anything up. Use conversational, friendly prose — not bullet points, not financial-report language.\n\n### Section 1 — The bottom line\nOne or two sentences. State the verdict and what it means in dollars.\nExample: \"At $130, NVIDIA is trading at roughly 60% below what our model thinks it's worth — the base estimate is $207 per share.\"\n\n### Section 2 — How we got here\nWalk through the DCF (discounted cash flow — a method that estimates what a business is worth based on the cash it will generate in the future) inputs in conversational terms. Cover:\n- Starting point: the company's free cash flow (FCF — the money left over after the business pays to keep itself running and invest in growth), SBC-adjusted (stock-based compensation — shares given to employees, which dilute existing shareholders)\n- Growth assumption: at what rate FCF is expected to grow over the next 5 years, and where that number came from (consensus analyst estimates, playbook ceiling, or fallback default)\n- Discount rate (WACC — weighted-average cost of capital, roughly \"the return investors require to own this stock\"): the rate used to convert future dollars into today's dollars\n- The mechanics: briefly explain the two-stage model (5 years of projected cash flows + a \"terminal value\" assuming slower perpetual growth thereafter)\n\n### Section 3 — What's driving the number most\nIdentify the 2–3 assumptions that move the intrinsic value most. Use the sensitivity note from the report. Show the impact in concrete dollar terms by referencing the bear/base/bull spread.\nExample: \"The biggest lever is the discount rate (WACC). Our base case uses 9%. A 1-point move either way shifts the estimated value by roughly $35 per share — which is why the bear case ($160) and bull case ($260) are so far apart.\"\n\n### Section 4 — What could go wrong\nSurface failure modes from the playbook if loaded. If not, synthesise 2–3 from the bear scenario narrative and the sensitivity dominant driver. Write each as a plain conversational sentence — what event happens, why it matters to the model. No bullet lists; flowing prose.\nExample: \"The main risk is that AI chip demand peaks earlier than expected. If that happens, NVIDIA's cash-flow growth could slow sharply — and a model that assumed 20% annual growth for five years would be far too optimistic. A second risk is margin compression if competitors catch up, which would shrink the cash flows we're projecting.\"\n\n### Section 5 — How much to trust this\nTranslate `meta.confidence` into plain language. Explain the reason (data completeness, playbook presence, whether any numbers were manually entered). If confidence is MEDIUM or LOW, name the specific gap.\nExample (HIGH): \"This estimate is based on four full years of cash-flow history, a detailed set of NVIDIA-specific assumptions (growth ceiling, discount rate), and all data came from live financial sources — so confidence is high.\"\nExample (MEDIUM): \"Confidence is medium because we had to manually provide the stock-compensation figure — that number is load-bearing in the model, so treat the estimate as a solid starting point rather than a precise answer.\"\n\n## Common rationalisations (must be in the skill — these must be pre-rebutted)\n| Rationalisation | Counter-argument |\n|---|---|\n| \"I'll fetch live prices to update the report\" | No. Explain uses the cached report only. Freshness is shown by the report date. If stale, tell the user to re-run /stock-signal and /stock-model. |\n| \"I'll add technical terms for precision\" | No. If a term needs a parenthetical to be understood, use only the parenthetical. The goal is comprehension, not completeness. |\n| \"I'll emit a JSON block so the output is auditable\" | No. This skill is legibility-only. The audit trail lives in the report JSON. |\n| \"I'll skip the FCF/SBC explanation since the user ran /stock-model already\" | No. Explain is for understanding how the number was built. Always explain the inputs even if the user has seen the model output. |\n\n## Invocation examples\n```\n/stock-explain NVDA\n/stock-explain META\n/stock-explain AAPL\n```\n\n## Dependencies\n- `reports/TICKER_YYYYMMDD.json` with `stages.signal` and `stages.model` populated\n- No MCP tools required\n- No external data fetches"
}
```

1	---
2	name: stock-signal
3	description: GARP signal analysis for a tech stock. Invoked as `/stock-signal TICKER`. Fetches live financials and estimates from the yfinance MCP, strips SBC, computes PEG/P/S, classifies AI layer, and outputs a structured SIGNAL OUTPUT block with a MODEL_READY flag. Use whenever the user asks to analyse a stock more deeply, investigate a ticker, check whether it passes GARP criteria, or after a `/stock-screen` PASS or WATCH result — even if they don't say "signal" or "GARP".
4	---
5	
6	# Signal — GARP Signal Analysis
7	
8	**Command:** `/stock-signal TICKER`
9	**Purpose:** GARP signal analysis. Fetches valuation ratios and financials from the yfinance MCP, classifies profit stage and AI layer, strips SBC from EPS, and emits a structured SIGNAL OUTPUT block with a MODEL_READY flag for downstream use by `/stock-model`.
10	
11	---
12	
13	
14	## 1. Identity
15	
16	- **Skill name:** stock-signal
17	- **Command:** `/stock-signal TICKER`
18	- **Purpose:** Produce a GARP signal verdict (BUY / WATCH / CAUTION) and a MODEL_READY flag for a single ticker, using yfinance data and a structured qualitative overlay.
19	
20	---
21	
22	## 2. Methodology
23	
24	Growth at a Reasonable Price (GARP) — a combination of:
25	
26	- **Clean EPS** (earnings after SBC removal) — SBC stripping is step zero, always
27	- **PEG ratio** (P/E ÷ forward EPS growth rate) — primary GARP metric for ESTABLISHED companies
28	- **P/S ratio** — primary metric for EMERGING companies where PEG is undefined
29	- **Rule of 40** (revenue growth % + FCF margin %) — supplementary filter for ESTABLISHED SaaS/software; N/A for hardware/semis
30	- **AI layer classification** — qualitative modifier applied to INFRASTRUCTURE / APPLICATION / MODEL / INCUMBENT companies

```json
{
  "file_path": "/Users/anton/src/stock-review/skills/stock-explain/SKILL.md",
  "content": "---\nname: stock-explain\ndescription: Plain-English walkthrough of a stock's valuation model output. Invoked as `/stock-explain TICKER`. Reads the latest cached report from `reports/TICKER_YYYYMMDD.json` and produces a five-section narrative — the bottom line, how the number was built, what assumptions drive it most, what could go wrong, and how much to trust it. No live data fetches; no file writes. Use whenever the user wants to understand what a valuation number means, how it was derived, or why the model says what it says — even if they just ask \"what does this mean?\", \"walk me through the model\", \"explain the number\", or \"how does the valuation work\". Designed for a reader with zero finance background.\n---\n\n# Explain — Plain-English Valuation Walkthrough\n\n**Command:** `/stock-explain TICKER`\n**Purpose:** Turn a cached model report into a friendly five-section narrative that any reader can follow — no finance knowledge required.\n\n---\n\n## 1. Identity\n\n- **Skill name:** stock-explain\n- **Command:** `/stock-explain TICKER`\n- **Purpose:** Read the latest `reports/TICKER_YYYYMMDD.json` and produce a plain-English walkthrough of the valuation. Read-only — no MCP calls, no file writes.\n\n---\n\n## 2. Methodology\n\nThis skill is a translator, not a calculator. All numbers come from the cached report; nothing is recomputed. The job is to render the model's inputs, outputs, and uncertainties in language that a smart non-investor can read in two minutes and actually understand.\n\nThe five sections correspond to the five questions an operator naturally asks when looking at a valuation number:\n1. *What does it say?*\n2. *How did it get there?*\n3. *What's really driving it?*\n4. *What could make it wrong?*\n5. *How much should I trust it?*\n\n---\n\n## 3. Input Schema\n\nAll inputs come from `reports/TICKER_YYYYMMDD.json`. No MCP calls. No internet access.\n\n| Field path | Used for |\n|---|---|\n| `stages.signal.verdict` | Direction of the signal (buy / watch / caution) |\n| `stages.signal.profit_stage` | Whether the company is established (profitable) or pre-profit |\n| `stages.signal.ai_layer` | Company's relationship to AI (infrastructure, application, etc.) |\n| `stages.signal.qualitative_note` | Any flags from the signal phase |\n| `stages.model.current_price` | Today's market price at the time of the model run |\n| `stages.model.fcf_ttm` | Trailing free cash flow (SBC-stripped) used as the model's starting point |\n| `stages.model.fcf_ttm_reported` | Reported free cash flow (before removing stock comp) — for comparison |\n| `stages.model.sbc_ttm` | Stock-based compensation removed from FCF |\n| `stages.model.fcf_margin_ttm` | Free cash flow as a percentage of revenue |\n| `stages.model.growth_rate` | Object: applied rate, whether it was capped, and the source of the cap |\n| `stages.model.base_wacc` | Discount rate used in the base scenario |\n| `stages.model.base_wacc_source` | Where the discount rate came from (playbook / generic / user) |\n| `stages.model.scenarios.bear/base/bull` | Three scenarios, each with IV per share, upside %, growth rate, WACC, terminal growth, narrative |\n| `stages.model.intrinsic_value_range` | Convenience object: bear / base / bull IV in dollars |\n| `stages.model.sensitivity` | Which input moves the IV most, and by how much |\n| `stages.model.playbook.loaded` | Whether ticker-specific assumptions were loaded |\n| `stages.model.playbook.failure_modes` | Company-specific downside scenarios (if playbook loaded) |\n| `meta.confidence` | HIGH / MEDIUM / LOW |\n\n---\n\n## 4. Execution Phases\n\n### Phase 1 — LOCATE\n\nFind the most recent report file for the requested ticker:\n\n```\nreports/TICKER_*.json  →  sort by date descending  →  take the first\n```\n\nIf no file is found, stop and say:\n\n> No report found for [TICKER]. Run `/stock-signal [TICKER]` first, then `/stock-model [TICKER]`, and try again.\n\nShow the report date at the top of the output so the reader knows how fresh the data is.\n\n### Phase 2 — VALIDATE\n\nCheck that both `stages.signal` and `stages.model` are present in the report.\n\n- If `stages.signal` is missing: \"The signal phase hasn't been run yet — run `/stock-signal [TICKER]` first.\"\n- If `stages.model` is missing: \"The model phase hasn't been run yet — run `/stock-model [TICKER]` first.\"\n\n### Phase 3 — EXPLAIN\n\nGenerate the five sections from the report data. The writing rules below are non-negotiable.\n\n### Phase 4 — EMIT\n\nPrint the output to the terminal. Do not write any files.\n\n---\n\n## 5. Writing Rules (read before drafting any section)\n\nThese rules exist because the goal is genuine comprehension, not the appearance of simplicity.\n\n**Every acronym and abbreviation must be spelled out on first use**, with a plain-English definition in parentheses. Examples:\n- FCF (free cash flow — the money the business generates after paying to keep itself running and grow)\n- DCF (discounted cash flow — a method for estimating what a business is worth based on the cash it will generate in the future)\n- WACC (weighted-average cost of capital — roughly, the annual return investors require to take on the risk of owning this stock)\n- SBC (stock-based compensation — shares or options given to employees as pay, which dilute existing shareholders)\n- IV (intrinsic value — our model's estimate of what the stock is actually worth per share)\n- TTM (trailing twelve months — the most recent full year of data)\n- PEG (price-to-earnings-to-growth ratio — a measure of whether a stock's price is reasonable given how fast earnings are growing)\n\n**Use conversational, flowing prose.** Not bullet points. Not tables. Not financial-report language (\"the company demonstrated robust free cash flow generation\"). Write the way a knowledgeable friend would explain something over coffee.\n\n**Show the impact of assumptions in concrete dollar terms.** Don't say \"WACC is a key driver.\" Say \"Dropping the discount rate by 1 percentage point would push the base estimate from $207 to roughly $240.\"\n\n**When the bear/base/bull spread is wide, acknowledge it honestly.** Don't paper over uncertainty.\n\n**Refer to the report date.** Never imply the data is live.\n\n---\n\n## 6. Output Format\n\n```\n/stock-explain [TICKER]  ·  Report date: YYYY-MM-DD\n━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━\n\n## The bottom line\n[1–2 sentences. Current price vs. base IV, expressed as a % gap. The signal verdict in plain English.]\n\n## How we got here\n[3–5 sentences. Walk through the model's inputs in order: starting FCF (and the SBC strip), growth assumption (and its source), discount rate, and the two-stage structure. Explain what \"discounting\" means.]\n\n## What's driving the number most\n[3–4 sentences. Name the 1–2 dominant inputs. Quantify their impact using the bear/base/bull spread or the sensitivity note. Explain why a wide spread is normal and what it means for the reader.]\n\n## What could go wrong\n[3–5 sentences. Flowing prose, not a list. If the playbook is loaded, use its failure_modes. If not, synthesise from the bear scenario narrative and the sensitivity dominant driver.]\n\n## How much to trust this\n[2–3 sentences. Translate meta.confidence into plain language. Explain the specific reason — data history, playbook presence, any manual inputs.]\n```\n\n---\n\n## 7. Section-by-Section Guidance\n\n### Section 1 — The bottom line\n\nState the current price, the base intrinsic value (IV), and the gap as a percentage. Mention the signal verdict in plain English (not \"BUY\" — say \"the model sees it as a buy\" or \"worth watching\").\n\n**Example:** \"At $130 per share, NVIDIA is trading at roughly 60% below what our model thinks it's worth today. The base estimate — our central, most-likely scenario — is $207 per share, which puts NVIDIA firmly in 'buy' territory by our criteria.\"\n\n### Section 2 — How we got here\n\nThis is the heart of the walkthrough. Cover these points in plain language:\n\n1. **Starting cash flow:** Name the cleaned-up free cash flow (FCF) figure. Briefly explain why it differs from the reported number — SBC (stock-based compensation — shares and options given to employees as pay) is subtracted because it's a real cost even though it doesn't appear as a cash payment.\n\n2. **Growth assumption:** State the 5-year growth rate used in the model. Explain where it came from — analyst consensus estimates, a ticker-specific ceiling from the playbook, or a generic fallback. If the rate was capped from a higher trailing rate, explain why: \"The trailing three-year growth rate was 45%, but we capped it at 25% using analyst consensus — raw historical rates often overstate what's sustainable.\"\n\n3. **Discount rate:** Name the WACC and explain it as \"the annual return investors demand to own this stock.\" Note whether it came from the playbook (company-specific) or a generic assumption.\n\n4. **The mechanics:** In one sentence, explain the two-stage structure: \"We projected those cash flows forward for five years, added a 'terminal value' (an estimate of what the business is worth from year six onward, assuming slower, steady-state growth), and discounted everything back to today's dollars.\"\n\n### Section 3 — What's driving the number most\n\nUse `stages.model.sensitivity` as the anchor. Name the dominant driver and quantify its effect. Then reference the bear/base/bull spread to show the reader what \"uncertainty\" looks like in dollar terms.\n\n**Example:** \"The biggest lever in this model is the discount rate — what we call the WACC (weighted-average cost of capital). Our base case uses 9%. If that rate drops to 8%, the estimated value climbs to about $240; if it rises to 10%, it falls to around $175. That's a $65-per-share swing from a single 2-point move — which is why the gap between our bear case ($160) and bull case ($260) is so wide. This isn't a flaw in the model; it's an honest reflection of the fact that valuations of high-growth companies are genuinely sensitive to interest-rate assumptions.\"\n\n### Section 4 — What could go wrong\n\nIf `stages.model.playbook.loaded` is true, use `playbook.failure_modes` as the primary source. If not, synthesise 2–3 risks from the bear scenario narrative and the sensitivity dominant driver.\n\nWrite this as flowing prose — two or three sentences that tell a story about what sequence of events would make the model's assumptions wrong. Avoid bullet points. Each risk should name: what event happens, why it matters to the model specifically (e.g., \"that would reduce the growth rate we projected\").\n\n**Example (playbook loaded):** \"The biggest risk is a cyclical slowdown in AI infrastructure spending. If cloud providers and hyperscalers pause their GPU buildout — as they did briefly in 2022 — NVIDIA's revenue could plateau, and the 20% annual growth rate our model assumes would quickly look too optimistic. A second risk is that NVIDIA's margins compress as competitors like AMD or custom-chip efforts from Google and Amazon eat into its pricing power. Thinner margins mean less free cash flow, and the model is built entirely on free cash flow projections.\"\n\n### Section 5 — How much to trust this\n\nTranslate `meta.confidence` simply:\n\n- **HIGH:** \"This is a high-confidence estimate. We have [N] years of cash-flow history, a set of NVIDIA-specific assumptions loaded from a playbook, and all numbers came directly from financial data feeds — nothing was filled in manually.\"\n- **MEDIUM:** \"Confidence here is medium. [Name the specific gap — e.g., 'We had to manually provide the stock-compensation figure' or 'The company only has two years of profitability history, which is a thin basis for a five-year projection.'] That doesn't make the estimate useless, but treat the range as wider than the model formally shows.\"\n- **LOW:** \"Confidence is low. [Name two gaps.] Use the bear/base/bull range as a rough compass rather than a precise answer.\"\n\n---\n\n## 8. Invocation Patterns\n\n```\n/stock-explain NVDA\n/stock-explain META\n/stock-explain AAPL\n/stock-explain MSFT\n```\n\n---\n\n## 9. Dependencies\n\n- `reports/TICKER_YYYYMMDD.json` with both `stages.signal` and `stages.model` populated\n- No MCP tools\n- No external network access\n\n---\n\n## 10. Common Rationalisations\n\n| What you might want to do | Why not to do it |\n|---|---|\n| Fetch live prices to make the output \"current\" | The skill explains the model that was run — live prices would mismatch the report's assumptions. If the report is stale, tell the user to re-run `/stock-signal` and `/stock-model`. |\n| Add technical terms back in for completeness or precision | If a term needs a parenthetical explanation, just use the parenthetical. The goal is comprehension, not completeness. |\n| Emit a JSON block for the audit trail | The audit trail is the report JSON file itself. This output is for human reading only. |\n| Skip the FCF/SBC explanation because the user already ran `/stock-model` | The whole point of `/stock-explain` is to build understanding from scratch. Always explain the inputs, regardless of what the user has already run. |\n| Use bullet points because they're cleaner | Bullets fragment a continuous argument into disconnected facts. Prose carries the reader through the logic. Use prose. |\n| Hedge every sentence (\"this may be\", \"it's possible that\") | The model has already handled uncertainty through the bear/base/bull range. Don't pile on additional verbal hedging — it reads as evasive rather than honest. State clearly what the model says, and clearly what the known risks are. |\n"
}

> AGENT

Six runs underway in parallel (NVDA, META, FAKE × with/without skill). While those run, here's what the evals are checking:

**Eval 0 — NVDA (full report with playbook):** Tests the happy path on a well-characterized ticker. Assertions check that all acronyms are defined (FCF, WACC, SBC, DCF), all five section headers appear, dollar figures are present, no JSON block leaks in, the report date shows, and the failure-modes section has substantive NVDA-specific content (not just boilerplate risk language).

**Eval 1 — META (full report, no playbook):** Same structural checks, plus an explicit assertion that there are no markdown bullet points — the skill requires flowing prose, and this eval verifies the skill doesn't fall back to lists when the prose discipline is harder.

**Eval 2 — FAKE (no report exists):** Tests the error path. Asserts that a clear "no report found" message appears, that the user is pointed to `/stock-signal` and `/stock-model`, and that none of the five valuation sections appear (no partial output that might confuse the reader).

The baseline runs (without skill) will show what Claude does by default — likely: competent, but probably uses bullets, technical terms without definitions, and omits the structured sections. That contrast is what validates the skill is earning its keep.

1	# JSON Schemas
2	
3	This document defines the JSON schemas used by skill-creator.
4	
5	---
6	
7	## evals.json
8	
9	Defines the evals for a skill. Located at `evals/evals.json` within the skill directory.
10	
11	```json
12	{
13	  "skill_name": "example-skill",
14	  "evals": [
15	    {
16	      "id": 1,
17	      "prompt": "User's example prompt",
18	      "expected_output": "Description of expected result",
19	      "files": ["evals/files/sample1.pdf"],
20	      "expectations": [
21	        "The output includes X",
22	        "The skill used script Y"
23	      ]
24	    }
25	  ]
26	}
27	```
28	
29	**Fields:**
30	- `skill_name`: Name matching the skill's frontmatter
31	- `evals[].id`: Unique integer identifier
32	- `evals[].prompt`: The task to execute
33	- `evals[].expected_output`: Human-readable description of success
34	- `evals[].files`: Optional list of input file paths (relative to skill root)
35	- `evals[].expectations`: List of verifiable statements
36	
37	---
38	
39	## history.json
40	
41	Tracks version progression in Improve mode. Located at workspace root.
42	
43	```json
44	{
45	  "started_at": "2026-01-15T10:30:00Z",
46	  "skill_name": "pdf",
47	  "current_best": "v2",
48	  "iterations": [
49	    {
50	      "version": "v0",
51	      "parent": null,
52	      "expectation_pass_rate": 0.65,
53	      "grading_result": "baseline",
54	      "is_current_best": false
55	    },
56	    {
57	      "version": "v1",
58	      "parent": "v0",
59	      "expectation_pass_rate": 0.75,
60	      "grading_result": "won",
61	      "is_current_best": false
62	    },
63	    {
64	      "version": "v2",
65	      "parent": "v1",
66	      "expectation_pass_rate": 0.85,
67	      "grading_result": "won",
68	      "is_current_best": true
69	    }
70	  ]
71	}
72	```
73	
74	**Fields:**
75	- `started_at`: ISO timestamp of when improvement started
76	- `skill_name`: Name of the skill being improved
77	- `current_best`: Version identifier of the best performer
78	- `iterations[].version`: Version identifier (v0, v1, ...)
79	- `iterations[].parent`: Parent version this was derived from
80	- `iterations[].expectation_pass_rate`: Pass rate from grading
81	- `iterations[].grading_result`: "baseline", "won", "lost", or "tie"
82	- `iterations[].is_current_best`: Whether this is the current best version
83	
84	---
85	
86	## grading.json
87	
88	Output from the grader agent. Located at `<run-dir>/grading.json`.
89	
90	```json
91	{
92	  "expectations": [
93	    {
94	      "text": "The output includes the name 'John Smith'",
95	      "passed": true,
96	      "evidence": "Found in transcript Step 3: 'Extracted names: John Smith, Sarah Johnson'"
97	    },
98	    {
99	      "text": "The spreadsheet has a SUM formula in cell B10",
100	      "passed": false,
101	      "evidence": "No spreadsheet was created. The output was a text file."
102	    }
103	  ],
104	  "summary": {
105	    "passed": 2,
106	    "failed": 1,
107	    "total": 3,
108	    "pass_rate": 0.67
109	  },
110	  "execution_metrics": {
111	    "tool_calls": {
112	      "Read": 5,
113	      "Write": 2,
114	      "Bash": 8
115	    },
116	    "total_tool_calls": 15,
117	    "total_steps": 6,
118	    "errors_encountered": 0,
119	    "output_chars": 12450,
120	    "transcript_chars": 3200
121	  },
122	  "timing": {
123	    "executor_duration_seconds": 165.0,
124	    "grader_duration_seconds": 26.0,
125	    "total_duration_seconds": 191.0
126	  },
127	  "claims": [
128	    {
129	      "claim": "The form has 12 fillable fields",
130	      "type": "factual",
131	      "verified": true,
132	      "evidence": "Counted 12 fields in field_info.json"
133	    }
134	  ],
135	  "user_notes_summary": {
136	    "uncertainties": ["Used 2023 data, may be stale"],
137	    "needs_review": [],
138	    "workarounds": ["Fell back to text overlay for non-fillable fields"]
139	  },
140	  "eval_feedback": {
141	    "suggestions": [
142	      {
143	        "assertion": "The output includes the name 'John Smith'",
144	        "reason": "A hallucinated document that mentions the name would also pass"
145	      }
146	    ],
147	    "overall": "Assertions check presence but not correctness."
148	  }
149	}
150	```
151	
152	**Fields:**
153	- `expectations[]`: Graded expectations with evidence
154	- `summary`: Aggregate pass/fail counts
155	- `execution_metrics`: Tool usage and output size (from executor's metrics.json)
156	- `timing`: Wall clock timing (from timing.json)
157	- `claims`: Extracted and verified claims from the output
158	- `user_notes_summary`: Issues flagged by the executor
159	- `eval_feedback`: (optional) Improvement suggestions for the evals, only present when the grader identifies issues worth raising
160	
161	---
162	
163	## metrics.json
164	
165	Output from the executor agent. Located at `<run-dir>/outputs/metrics.json`.
166	
167	```json
168	{
169	  "tool_calls": {
170	    "Read": 5,
171	    "Write": 2,
172	    "Bash": 8,
173	    "Edit": 1,
174	    "Glob": 2,
175	    "Grep": 0
176	  },
177	  "total_tool_calls": 18,
178	  "total_steps": 6,
179	  "files_created": ["filled_form.pdf", "field_values.json"],
180	  "errors_encountered": 0,
181	  "output_chars": 12450,
182	  "transcript_chars": 3200
183	}
184	```
185	
186	**Fields:**
187	- `tool_calls`: Count per tool type
188	- `total_tool_calls`: Sum of all tool calls
189	- `total_steps`: Number of major execution steps
190	- `files_created`: List of output files created
191	- `errors_encountered`: Number of errors during execution
192	- `output_chars`: Total character count of output files
193	- `transcript_chars`: Character count of transcript
194	
195	---
196	
197	## timing.json
198	
199	Wall clock timing for a run. Located at `<run-dir>/timing.json`.
200	
201	**How to capture:** When a subagent task completes, the task notification includes `total_tokens` and `duration_ms`. Save these immediately — they are not persisted anywhere else and cannot be recovered after the fact.
202	
203	```json
204	{
205	  "total_tokens": 84852,
206	  "duration_ms": 23332,
207	  "total_duration_seconds": 23.3,
208	  "executor_start": "2026-01-15T10:30:00Z",
209	  "executor_end": "2026-01-15T10:32:45Z",
210	  "executor_duration_seconds": 165.0,
211	  "grader_start": "2026-01-15T10:32:46Z",
212	  "grader_end": "2026-01-15T10:33:12Z",
213	  "grader_duration_seconds": 26.0
214	}
215	```
216	
217	---
218	
219	## benchmark.json
220	
221	Output from Benchmark mode. Located at `benchmarks/<timestamp>/benchmark.json`.
222	
223	```json
224	{
225	  "metadata": {
226	    "skill_name": "pdf",
227	    "skill_path": "/path/to/pdf",
228	    "executor_model": "claude-sonnet-4-20250514",
229	    "analyzer_model": "most-capable-model",
230	    "timestamp": "2026-01-15T10:30:00Z",
231	    "evals_run": [1, 2, 3],
232	    "runs_per_configuration": 3
233	  },
234	
235	  "runs": [
236	    {
237	      "eval_id": 1,
238	      "eval_name": "Ocean",
239	      "configuration": "with_skill",
240	      "run_number": 1,
241	      "result": {
242	        "pass_rate": 0.85,
243	        "passed": 6,
244	        "failed": 1,

253	      ],

257	      ]
258	    }
259	  ],
260

263	      "pass_rate": {"mean": 0.85, "stddev": 0.05, "min": 0.80, "max": 0.90},
264	      "time_seconds": {"mean": 45.0, "stddev": 12.0, "min": 32.0, "max": 58.0},
265	      "tokens": {"mean": 3800, "stddev": 400, "min": 3200, "max": 4100}
266	    },

269	      "time_seconds": {"mean": 32.0, "stddev": 8.0, "min": 24.0, "max": 42.0},
270	      "tokens": {"mean": 2100, "stddev": 300, "min": 1800, "max": 2500}
271	    },

275	      "tokens": "+1700"
276	    }

281	    "Eval 3 shows high variance (50% ± 40%) - may be flaky or model-dependent",
282	    "Without-skill runs consistently fail on table extraction expectations",
283	    "Skill adds 13s average execution time but improves pass rate by 50%"
284	  ]
285	}
286	```
287	
288	**Fields:**
289	- `metadata`: Information about the benchmark run
290	  - `skill_name`: Name of the skill
291	  - `timestamp`: When the benchmark was run
292	  - `evals_run`: List of eval names or IDs
293	  - `runs_per_configuration`: Number of runs per config (e.g. 3)
294	- `runs[]`: Individual run results
295	  - `eval_id`: Numeric eval identifier
296	  - `eval_name`: Human-readable eval name (used as section header in the viewer)
297	  - `configuration`: Must be `"with_skill"` or `"without_skill"` (the viewer uses this exact string for grouping and color coding)
298	  - `run_number`: Integer run number (1, 2, 3...)
299	  - `result`: Nested object with `pass_rate`, `passed`, `total`, `time_seconds`, `tokens`, `errors`
300	- `run_summary`: Statistical aggregates per configuration
301	  - `with_skill` / `without_skill`: Each contains `pass_rate`, `time_seconds`, `tokens` objects with `mean` and `stddev` fields
302	  - `delta`: Difference strings like `"+0.50"`, `"+13.0"`, `"+1700"`
303	- `notes`: Freeform observations from the analyzer
304	
305	**Important:** The viewer reads these field names exactly. Using `config` instead of `configuration`, or putting `pass_rate` at the top level of a run instead of nested under `result`, will cause the viewer to show empty/zero values. Always reference this schema when generating benchmark.json manually.

1	/stock-explain NVDA  ·  Report date: 2026-05-20
2	━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
3	
4	## The bottom line
5	
6	At $220.09 per share, NVIDIA is trading at roughly 37% below what our model thinks it's worth in the base case. The base intrinsic value (IV — our model's estimate of what the stock is actually worth per share) is $351.65, which puts NVIDIA firmly in "buy" territory by our criteria. However, there's an important nuance: the current price sits between our worst-case ($139.60) and base-case estimates, meaning we're inside that range rather than safely below the bear floor — so while the model says buy, it's not a slam-dunk margin-of-safety situation.
7	
8	## How we got here
9	
10	The model's starting point is NVIDIA's FCF (free cash flow — the money the business generates after paying to keep itself running and grow) over the most recent full year. The reported figure was $96.7 billion, but we strip out SBC (stock-based compensation — shares and options given to employees as pay, which dilute existing shareholders) of $6.4 billion, bringing the cleaned-up FCF to $90.3 billion. That subtraction matters because SBC is a real cost to shareholders even though it doesn't leave the company as a cash payment. NVIDIA's FCF margin (free cash flow as a percentage of revenue) after this adjustment is 41.8%, which is extraordinarily high and reflects the pricing power the company currently enjoys on its data-centre chips.
11	
12	For the growth assumption, the model applies a 35% annual growth rate in the base scenario for the first five years. That number came from the NVIDIA-specific playbook — a set of hand-crafted assumptions tailored to this company. The three-year trailing FCF compound annual growth rate was actually 335%, but that number is dominated by the explosive 2023-to-2025 surge in AI chip demand and would be absurd to project forward. The playbook caps the base-case rate at 35%, which reflects a more sustainable (though still aggressive) continued buildout. The discount rate — called the WACC (weighted-average cost of capital — roughly, the annual return investors require to take on the risk of owning this stock) — is set at 9% in the base case, also sourced from the playbook rather than a generic assumption.
13	
14	Mechanically, the model projects those cash flows forward for five years, then adds a terminal value (an estimate of what the business is worth from year six onward, assuming slower, steady-state growth of 2.5% per year in the base case), and discounts everything back to today's dollars using the WACC. That process — projecting and discounting future cash flows — is what a DCF (discounted cash flow) valuation means: a bet on what all future profits are worth right now.
15	
16	## What's driving the number most
17	
18	The single biggest lever in this model is the WACC. According to the sensitivity analysis, a move of 100 basis points (1 percentage point) in the WACC shifts the base intrinsic value by approximately 17%. In concrete terms: our base case uses 9% and produces $351.65 per share. If that rate drops to 8% — the bull-case assumption — the estimate climbs to around $412. If it rises to 10% — the bear-case assumption — the estimate falls toward $300. That alone is a $100+ per-share swing. Combined with the wide difference in growth rate assumptions (17.5% per year in the bear versus 44% in the bull) and terminal growth (1.5% versus 3.5%), the bear-to-bull range is enormous: $139.60 to $712.52. That's not a flaw in the model — it's an honest reflection of the genuine uncertainty in projecting how fast a company this large can sustain growth, and what required returns will look like over a five-year horizon.
19	
20	## What could go wrong
21	
22	The most immediate risk is a deceleration in hyperscaler capital expenditure spending. NVIDIA derives roughly 70% of its revenue from the big four cloud providers — Microsoft, Google, Amazon, and Meta — so if any two of them announce cuts to AI infrastructure budgets in the same quarter, NVIDIA's revenue would feel it almost immediately, and the 35% annual growth rate the base case assumes would suddenly look far too optimistic.
23	
24	A second distinct risk is the gradual displacement of NVIDIA's chips by custom silicon. ASIC (application-specific integrated circuit) inference chips — designed by the likes of Broadcom and Marvell for specific AI workloads — are already taking share, and if they exceed roughly 25% of AI inference workloads by 2027, it removes one of the key tailwinds that underpins the CUDA (NVIDIA's proprietary software platform that locks AI workloads to NVIDIA hardware) lock-in thesis.
25	
26	Export controls add a third dimension: if the US government tightens restrictions to cover the successor chips to the H20 (NVIDIA's China-market GPU), it eliminates the residual China revenue stream and signals that further restrictions are politically viable. Finally, there is a structural TAM (total addressable market — the total pool of revenue a company could theoretically capture) ceiling risk flagged in the signal phase: the data-centre GPU market is estimated at roughly $300 billion, and NVIDIA's annualised data-centre revenue already represents about 63% penetration. The bull case depends on physical AI and robotics opening a second major market — if that doesn't materialise, the runway for continued growth is genuinely constrained.
27	
28	## How much to trust this
29	
30	Confidence here is medium. The report draws on multiple years of cash-flow history and uses a set of NVIDIA-specific assumptions loaded from a dedicated playbook, which is a meaningful quality upgrade over a generic model. However, the consensus five-year analyst growth estimate was not available in the data feed at the time of this run, so the base growth rate of 35% comes from the playbook rather than a quantitative consensus anchor — which means one key calibration input rests on qualitative judgement rather than aggregated forecasts. The bear-to-bull spread of $573 per share ($139.60 to $712.52) should be read as a genuine reflection of that uncertainty: use the base estimate as a directional reference point, not as a precise target.
31
