1	---
2	name: stock-explain
3	description: Plain-English walkthrough of a stock's valuation model output. Invoked as `/stock-explain TICKER`. Reads the latest cached report from `reports/TICKER_YYYYMMDD.json` and produces a five-section narrative — the bottom line, how the number was built, what assumptions drive it most, what could go wrong, and how much to trust it. No live data fetches; no file writes. Use whenever the user wants to understand what a valuation number means, how it was derived, or why the model says what it says — even if they just ask "what does this mean?", "walk me through the model", "explain the number", or "how does the valuation work". Designed for a reader with zero finance background.
4	---
5	
6	# Explain — Plain-English Valuation Walkthrough
7	
8	**Command:** `/stock-explain TICKER`
9	**Purpose:** Turn a cached model report into a friendly five-section narrative that any reader can follow — no finance knowledge required.
10	
11	---
12	
13	## 1. Identity
14	
15	- **Skill name:** stock-explain
16	- **Command:** `/stock-explain TICKER`
17	- **Purpose:** Read the latest `reports/TICKER_YYYYMMDD.json` and produce a plain-English walkthrough of the valuation. Read-only — no MCP calls, no file writes.
18	
19	---
20	
21	## 2. Methodology
22	
23	This skill is a translator, not a calculator. All numbers come from the cached report; nothing is recomputed. The job is to render the model's inputs, outputs, and uncertainties in language that a smart non-investor can read in two minutes and actually understand.
24	
25	The five sections correspond to the five questions an operator naturally asks when looking at a valuation number:
26	1. *What does it say?*
27	2. *How did it get there?*
28	3. *What's really driving it?*
29	4. *What could make it wrong?*
30	5. *How much should I trust it?*
31	
32	---
33	
34	## 3. Input Schema
35	
36	All inputs come from `reports/TICKER_YYYYMMDD.json`. No MCP calls. No internet access.
37	
38	| Field path | Used for |
39	|---|---|
40	| `stages.signal.verdict` | Direction of the signal (buy / watch / caution) |
41	| `stages.signal.profit_stage` | Whether the company is established (profitable) or pre-profit |
42	| `stages.signal.ai_layer` | Company's relationship to AI (infrastructure, application, etc.) |
43	| `stages.signal.qualitative_note` | Any flags from the signal phase |
44	| `stages.model.current_price` | Today's market price at the time of the model run |
45	| `stages.model.fcf_ttm` | Trailing free cash flow (SBC-stripped) used as the model's starting point |
46	| `stages.model.fcf_ttm_reported` | Reported free cash flow (before removing stock comp) — for comparison |
47	| `stages.model.sbc_ttm` | Stock-based compensation removed from FCF |
48	| `stages.model.fcf_margin_ttm` | Free cash flow as a percentage of revenue |
49	| `stages.model.growth_rate` | Object: applied rate, whether it was capped, and the source of the cap |
50	| `stages.model.base_wacc` | Discount rate used in the base scenario |
51	| `stages.model.base_wacc_source` | Where the discount rate came from (playbook / generic / user) |
52	| `stages.model.scenarios.bear/base/bull` | Three scenarios, each with IV per share, upside %, growth rate, WACC, terminal growth, narrative |
53	| `stages.model.intrinsic_value_range` | Convenience object: bear / base / bull IV in dollars |
54	| `stages.model.sensitivity` | Which input moves the IV most, and by how much |
55	| `stages.model.playbook.loaded` | Whether ticker-specific assumptions were loaded |
56	| `stages.model.playbook.failure_modes` | Company-specific downside scenarios (if playbook loaded) |
57	| `meta.confidence` | HIGH / MEDIUM / LOW |
58	
59	---
60	
61	## 4. Execution Phases
62	
63	### Phase 1 — LOCATE
64	
65	Find the most recent report file for the requested ticker:
66	
67	```
68	reports/TICKER_*.json  →  sort by date descending  →  take the first
69	```
70	
71	If no file is found, stop and say:
72	
73	> No report found for [TICKER]. Run `/stock-signal [TICKER]` first, then `/stock-model [TICKER]`, and try again.
74	
75	Show the report date at the top of the output so the reader knows how fresh the data is.
76	
77	### Phase 2 — VALIDATE
78	
79	Check that both `stages.signal` and `stages.model` are present in the report.
80	
81	- If `stages.signal` is missing: "The signal phase hasn't been run yet — run `/stock-signal [TICKER]` first."
82	- If `stages.model` is missing: "The model phase hasn't been run yet — run `/stock-model [TICKER]` first."
83	
84	### Phase 3 — EXPLAIN
85	
86	Generate the five sections from the report data. The writing rules below are non-negotiable.
87	
88	### Phase 4 — EMIT
89	
90	Print the output to the terminal. Do not write any files.
91	
92	---
93	
94	## 5. Writing Rules (read before drafting any section)
95	
96	These rules exist because the goal is genuine comprehension, not the appearance of simplicity.
97	
98	**Every acronym and abbreviation must be spelled out on first use**, with a plain-English definition in parentheses. Examples:
99	- FCF (free cash flow — the money the business generates after paying to keep itself running and grow)
100	- DCF (discounted cash flow — a method for estimating what a business is worth based on the cash it will generate in the future)
101	- WACC (weighted-average cost of capital — roughly, the annual return investors require to take on the risk of owning this stock)
102	- SBC (stock-based compensation — shares or options given to employees as pay, which dilute existing shareholders)
103	- IV (intrinsic value — our model's estimate of what the stock is actually worth per share)
104	- TTM (trailing twelve months — the most recent full year of data)
105	- PEG (price-to-earnings-to-growth ratio — a measure of whether a stock's price is reasonable given how fast earnings are growing)
106	
107	**Mix prose and bullets where each works best.** Use flowing prose for explanations that require connecting logic — the kind of thing a knowledgeable friend would say over coffee. Use bullet points for genuine lists where items are parallel and don't need to flow into each other (failure modes, a set of key inputs, multiple scenarios). Don't force everything into bullets, and don't force everything into prose. Avoid financial-report language ("the company demonstrated robust free cash flow generation").
108	
109	**Show the impact of assumptions in concrete dollar terms.** Don't say "WACC is a key driver." Say "Dropping the discount rate by 1 percentage point would push the base estimate from $207 to roughly $240."
110	
111	**When the bear/base/bull spread is wide, acknowledge it honestly.** Don't paper over uncertainty.
112	
113	**Refer to the report date.** Never imply the data is live.
114	
115	---
116	
117	## 6. Output Format
118	
119	```
120	/stock-explain [TICKER]  ·  Report date: YYYY-MM-DD
121	━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
122	
123	## The bottom line
124	[1–2 sentences. Current price vs. base IV, expressed as a % gap. The signal verdict in plain English.]
125	
126	## How we got here
127	[3–5 sentences. Walk through the model's inputs in order: starting FCF (and the SBC strip), growth assumption (and its source), discount rate, and the two-stage structure. Explain what "discounting" means.]
128	
129	## What's driving the number most
130	[3–5 sentences. Name the 1–2 dominant inputs. Quantify their impact using the bear/base/bull spread or the sensitivity note. Explain why a wide spread is normal. Then — crucially — compute and state the implied year-5 free cash flow at the base scenario growth rate: this tells the reader what the company would need to be generating for the base estimate to hold, and makes the growth assumption concrete and assessable.]
131	
132	## What could go wrong
133	[2–3 bullet points or short paragraphs, one per risk. Use the playbook failure_modes if present; otherwise synthesise from the bear scenario. Each risk should name: what happens, and why it would break the model's growth assumption.]
134	
135	## How much to trust this
136	[2–3 sentences. Translate meta.confidence into plain language. Explain the specific reason — data history, playbook presence, any manual inputs.]
137	```
138	
139	---
140	
141	## 7. Section-by-Section Guidance
142	
143	### Section 1 — The bottom line
144	
145	State the current price, the base intrinsic value (IV), and the gap as a percentage. Mention the signal verdict in plain English (not "BUY" — say "the model sees it as a buy" or "worth watching").
146	
147	**Example:** "At $130 per share, NVIDIA is trading at roughly 60% below what our model thinks it's worth today. The base estimate — our central, most-likely scenario — is $207 per share, which puts NVIDIA firmly in 'buy' territory by our criteria."
148	
149	### Section 2 — How we got here
150	
151	This is the heart of the walkthrough. Cover these points in plain language:
152	
153	1. **Starting cash flow:** Name the cleaned-up free cash flow (FCF) figure. Briefly explain why it differs from the reported number — SBC (stock-based compensation — shares and options given to employees as pay) is subtracted because it's a real cost even though it doesn't appear as a cash payment.
154	
155	2. **Growth assumption:** State the 5-year growth rate used in the model. Explain where it came from — analyst consensus estimates, a ticker-specific ceiling from the playbook, or a generic fallback. If the rate was capped from a higher trailing rate, explain why: "The trailing three-year growth rate was 45%, but we capped it at 25% using analyst consensus — raw historical rates often overstate what's sustainable."
156	
157	3. **Discount rate:** Name the WACC and explain it as "the annual return investors demand to own this stock." Note whether it came from the playbook (company-specific) or a generic assumption.
158	
159	4. **The mechanics:** In one sentence, explain the two-stage structure: "We projected those cash flows forward for five years, added a 'terminal value' (an estimate of what the business is worth from year six onward, assuming slower, steady-state growth), and discounted everything back to today's dollars."
160	
161	### Section 3 — What's driving the number most
162	
163	Use `stages.model.sensitivity` as the anchor. Name the dominant driver and quantify its effect. Then reference the bear/base/bull spread to show the reader what "uncertainty" looks like in dollar terms.
164	
165	**Required: implied year-5 FCF.** After the sensitivity analysis, compute and state what the base scenario's growth rate implies about how much free cash flow the company needs to be generating in five years. Use these fields:
166	
167	- `scenarios.base.y1_fcf` — the explicit year-1 FCF projection
168	- `scenarios.base.y2_5_cagr` — the annual growth rate for years 2–5
169	- Implied year-5 FCF = `y1_fcf × (1 + y2_5_cagr)^4`
170	
171	If `y1_fcf` is not present, fall back to: `fcf_ttm × (1 + growth_rate.applied_base_cagr)^5`
172	
173	Frame it as a reality check — compare it to today's FCF to show the scale of growth the model is betting on:
174	
175	**Example:** "To put those numbers in concrete terms: the model projects NVIDIA's free cash flow — its actual cash profits after expenses — will grow from $90 billion today to roughly $690 billion by 2031. That's roughly a 7.5× increase from today's already-exceptional base. Whether that's plausible depends on your view of AI infrastructure demand over the next five years; if you think it's too aggressive, you can re-run the model with a lower growth rate and see how the estimate changes."
176	
177	This is the most useful anchor for the reader: it translates the abstract % growth rate into an absolute earnings target that can be sanity-checked against industry size, comparable companies, or historical precedent.
178	
179	### Section 4 — What could go wrong
180	
181	If `stages.model.playbook.loaded` is true, use `playbook.failure_modes` as the primary source. If not, synthesise 2–3 risks from the bear scenario narrative and the sensitivity dominant driver.
182	
183	Write each risk as a short bullet or short paragraph — one risk per item. Each risk should name: (1) what event happens, and (2) why it breaks the model's specific growth assumption. Don't chain all risks into a single paragraph; separating them makes each one easier to evaluate.
184	
185	**Example (playbook loaded):**
186	- **AI capex slowdown:** If cloud providers cut GPU spending — as happened briefly in 2022 — NVIDIA's revenue could plateau and the 35% annual growth the base case assumes would quickly look too optimistic.
187	- **Custom-chip displacement:** ASIC chips built by Google, Amazon, and others are already taking share in AI inference. If they exceed 25% of AI workloads by 2027, it removes one of the main tailwinds behind NVIDIA's pricing power.
188	- **Export controls:** If the US government tightens rules to cover NVIDIA's next-generation China chips, it eliminates residual China revenue and signals that further restrictions are politically viable.
189	
190	### Section 5 — How much to trust this
191	
192	Translate `meta.confidence` simply:
193	
194	- **HIGH:** "This is a high-confidence estimate. We have [N] years of cash-flow history, a set of NVIDIA-specific assumptions loaded from a playbook, and all numbers came directly from financial data feeds — nothing was filled in manually."
195	- **MEDIUM:** "Confidence here is medium. [Name the specific gap — e.g., 'We had to manually provide the stock-compensation figure' or 'The company only has two years of profitability history, which is a thin basis for a five-year projection.'] That doesn't make the estimate useless, but treat the range as wider than the model formally shows."
196	- **LOW:** "Confidence is low. [Name two gaps.] Use the bear/base/bull range as a rough compass rather than a precise answer."
197	
198	---
199	
200	## 8. Invocation Patterns
201	
202	```
203	/stock-explain NVDA
204	/stock-explain META
205	/stock-explain AAPL
206	/stock-explain MSFT
207	```
208	
209	---
210	
211	## 9. Dependencies
212	
213	- `reports/TICKER_YYYYMMDD.json` with both `stages.signal` and `stages.model` populated
214	- No MCP tools
215	- No external network access
216	
217	---
218	
219	## 10. Common Rationalisations
220	
221	| What you might want to do | Why not to do it |
222	|---|---|
223	| Fetch live prices to make the output "current" | The skill explains the model that was run — live prices would mismatch the report's assumptions. If the report is stale, tell the user to re-run `/stock-signal` and `/stock-model`. |
224	| Add technical terms back in for completeness or precision | If a term needs a parenthetical explanation, just use the parenthetical. The goal is comprehension, not completeness. |
225	| Emit a JSON block for the audit trail | The audit trail is the report JSON file itself. This output is for human reading only. |
226	| Skip the FCF/SBC explanation because the user already ran `/stock-model` | The whole point of `/stock-explain` is to build understanding from scratch. Always explain the inputs, regardless of what the user has already run. |
227	| Skip the implied year-5 FCF calculation | This is the most concrete way to sanity-check the growth assumption. Always compute and state it in the "What's driving the number most" section. |
228	| Hedge every sentence ("this may be", "it's possible that") | The model has already handled uncertainty through the bear/base/bull range. Don't pile on additional verbal hedging — it reads as evasive rather than honest. State clearly what the model says, and clearly what the known risks are. |
229	
```

{
  "file_path": "/Users/anton/src/stock-review/skills/stock-explain/SKILL.md",
  "content": "---\nname: stock-explain\ndescription: Plain-English walkthrough of a stock's valuation model output. Invoked as `/stock-explain TICKER`. Reads the latest cached report from `reports/TICKER_YYYYMMDD.json` and produces a friendly narrative covering five questions — what the number says, how it was built, what drives it, what could break it, and how much to trust it. No live data fetches; no file writes. Use whenever the user wants to understand what a valuation number means, how it was derived, or why the model says what it says — even if they just ask \"what does this mean?\", \"walk me through the model\", \"explain the number\", or \"how does the valuation work\". Designed for a reader with zero finance background.\n---\n\n# Explain — Plain-English Valuation Walkthrough\n\n**Command:** `/stock-explain TICKER`\n**Purpose:** Turn a cached model report into a friendly narrative that any reader can follow — no finance knowledge required.\n\n---\n\n## 1. Identity\n\n- **Skill name:** stock-explain\n- **Command:** `/stock-explain TICKER`\n- **Purpose:** Read the latest `reports/TICKER_YYYYMMDD.json` and produce a plain-English walkthrough of the valuation. Read-only — no MCP calls, no file writes.\n\n---\n\n## 2. Methodology\n\nThis skill is a translator, not a calculator. All numbers come from the cached report; nothing is recomputed except one specific anchor (implied year-5 free cash flow — see Section 6). The job is to render the model's inputs, outputs, and uncertainties in language that a smart non-investor can read in three minutes and actually understand.\n\nThe output answers five questions a thoughtful reader naturally asks:\n\n1. *What does the number say?* — the bottom line\n2. *How did we get there?* — model inputs and mechanics\n3. *What's really driving it?* — sensitivity and the bet being made\n4. *What could break it?* — failure modes\n5. *How much should I trust it?* — confidence with qualitative context\n\nUse these as your skeleton. Choose section headings that fit the ticker rather than reusing the same five phrases every time — a generic template reads like boilerplate, and the goal is something a friend wrote, not something a form produced.\n\n---\n\n## 3. Input Schema\n\nAll inputs come from `reports/TICKER_YYYYMMDD.json`. No MCP calls. No internet access.\n\n| Field path | Used for |\n|---|---|\n| `stages.signal.verdict` | Direction of the signal (buy / watch / caution) |\n| `stages.signal.profit_stage` | Whether the company is established (profitable) or pre-profit |\n| `stages.signal.ai_layer` | Company's relationship to AI (infrastructure, application, etc.) |\n| `stages.signal.qualitative_note` | Any flags from the signal phase |\n| `stages.model.current_price` | Today's market price at the time of the model run |\n| `stages.model.fcf_ttm` | Trailing free cash flow (SBC-stripped) used as the model's starting point |\n| `stages.model.fcf_ttm_reported` | Reported free cash flow (before removing stock comp) — for comparison |\n| `stages.model.sbc_ttm` | Stock-based compensation removed from FCF |\n| `stages.model.fcf_margin_ttm` | Free cash flow as a percentage of revenue |\n| `stages.model.growth_rate` | Object: applied rate, whether it was capped, and the source of the cap |\n| `stages.model.base_wacc` | Discount rate used in the base scenario |\n| `stages.model.base_wacc_source` | Where the discount rate came from (playbook / generic / user) |\n| `stages.model.scenarios.bear/base/bull` | Three scenarios, each with IV per share, upside %, growth rate, WACC, terminal growth, narrative |\n| `stages.model.intrinsic_value_range` | Convenience object: bear / base / bull IV in dollars |\n| `stages.model.sensitivity` | Which input moves the IV most, and by how much |\n| `stages.model.playbook.loaded` | Whether ticker-specific assumptions were loaded |\n| `stages.model.playbook.failure_modes` | Company-specific downside scenarios (if playbook loaded) |\n| `meta.confidence` | HIGH / MEDIUM / LOW |\n\n---\n\n## 4. Execution Phases\n\n### Phase 1 — LOCATE\n\nFind the most recent report file for the requested ticker:\n\n```\nreports/TICKER_*.json  →  sort by date descending  →  take the first\n```\n\nIf no file is found, stop and say:\n\n> No report found for [TICKER]. Run `/stock-signal [TICKER]` first, then `/stock-model [TICKER]`, and try again.\n\nShow the report date near the top of the output so the reader knows how fresh the data is.\n\n### Phase 2 — VALIDATE\n\nCheck that both `stages.signal` and `stages.model` are present in the report.\n\n- If `stages.signal` is missing: \"The signal phase hasn't been run yet — run `/stock-signal [TICKER]` first.\"\n- If `stages.model` is missing: \"The model phase hasn't been run yet — run `/stock-model [TICKER]` first.\"\n\n### Phase 3 — EXPLAIN\n\nDraft the narrative according to the writing principles in Section 5 and the content requirements in Section 6.\n\n### Phase 4 — EMIT\n\nPrint the output to the terminal. Do not write any files.\n\n---\n\n## 5. Writing Principles\n\nThese shape the *craft* of the explanation. Read them before drafting, not after.\n\n**Define every acronym and abbreviation on first use.** Put a plain-English gloss in parentheses. Case doesn't matter — what matters is that a smart non-investor never hits a term they can't decode. Common ones to define:\n\n- free cash flow (FCF) — the money the business generates after paying to keep itself running and grow\n- discounted cash flow (DCF) — a method for estimating what a business is worth based on the cash it will generate in the future\n- weighted-average cost of capital (WACC) — roughly, the annual return investors require to take on the risk of owning this stock\n- stock-based compensation (SBC) — shares or options given to employees as pay, which dilute existing shareholders\n- intrinsic value (IV) — our model's estimate of what the stock is actually worth per share\n- trailing twelve months (TTM) — the most recent full year of data\n\n**Sound like a knowledgeable friend, not a form.** Mix flowing prose with bullets, short tables, and per-scenario callouts where each works best. Use prose when the logic has to connect (\"we capped growth at 25% because…\"); use bullets when items are genuinely parallel (failure modes, scenario takeaways). Avoid analyst-report mannerisms (\"the company demonstrated robust free cash flow generation\").\n\n**Choose headings that fit the ticker.** Suggested phrasings — \"The bottom line\", \"How we got here\", \"What's driving the number most\", \"What could go wrong\", \"How much to trust this\" — are fine but not mandatory. If a different phrasing serves the specific story better, use it. The five *questions* in Section 2 are what's load-bearing, not the exact words.\n\n**Quantify every assumption claim.** Don't say \"WACC is a key driver.\" Say \"Dropping the discount rate by 1 percentage point would push the base estimate from $207 to roughly $240\" — pull the dollar impact from `stages.model.sensitivity` or compute it from the scenario spread. The reader should always be able to see what would change the answer and by how much.\n\n**Honour the bear/base/bull spread.** A wide spread is a fact about the uncertainty in the bet, not a flaw to hide. State it honestly and explain *why* it's wide (usually: the growth rate or WACC has high uncertainty).\n\n**Refer to the report date.** Never imply the data is live. If the report is more than a few weeks old, note it.\n\n---\n\n## 6. Content Requirements\n\nThese are the elements the output must contain. The order is flexible; the elements are not.\n\n### A. Bottom line (1–2 sentences near the top)\n\nCurrent price, base intrinsic value, and the gap as a percentage. Translate the signal verdict to plain language (\"the model sees it as a buy\" rather than \"BUY\"). Show the report date here or in the heading area.\n\n### B. How the number was built\n\nWalk through the inputs in the order they enter the model:\n\n1. **Starting cash flow** — name the cleaned-up FCF figure, briefly explain why it differs from the reported number (SBC is subtracted because it's a real cost even though it doesn't show up as a cash payment).\n2. **Growth assumption** — state the 5-year rate, where it came from (analyst consensus, ticker-specific playbook ceiling, or a generic fallback), and any cap that was applied. Explain *why* caps exist when relevant (\"raw historical rates often overstate what's sustainable\").\n3. **Discount rate (WACC)** — name it, explain it in plain terms, note whether it's company-specific or generic.\n4. **The mechanics** — one sentence: \"We project five years of free cash flow, add a terminal value (an estimate of what the business is worth from year six onward at a slower steady state), and discount everything back to today's dollars.\"\n\n### C. What's driving the number — including two REQUIRED elements\n\n**Required: WACC sensitivity with the interest-rate angle.** Use `stages.model.sensitivity` to anchor a quantified statement of how much a 1 percentage point change in WACC moves the per-share value. Then connect it to the macro lens the reader actually cares about: \"If long-term interest rates rise, WACC rises with them — and the model's estimate would compress accordingly.\" This is the single most-asked question from non-finance readers; never skip it.\n\n**Required: implied year-5 free cash flow.** After explaining the sensitivity, compute and state what the base scenario's growth rate implies for the company's free cash flow in five years. This is the only number you compute yourself — everything else comes straight from the report.\n\n- Use `scenarios.base.y1_fcf × (1 + scenarios.base.y2_5_cagr)^4`.\n- Fall back to `fcf_ttm × (1 + growth_rate.applied_base_cagr)^5` if `y1_fcf` is missing.\n- State it with a future year (\"by 2031, ~$691 billion in free cash flow\") and a comparison to today (\"roughly 7.5× the $90 billion the business generates now\").\n- Frame it as a reality check the reader can sanity-test against industry size or comparable companies. This anchor is what makes the growth assumption *concrete*.\n\n**Optional but encouraged: per-scenario narratives.** Each scenario in the report has its own narrative field. Surface them with framing like \"what has to go right\" / \"what has to go wrong\" / \"what the base case assumes\" — this makes the bear/base/bull range mean something rather than being three numbers in a row.\n\n### D. What could go wrong\n\nIf `stages.model.playbook.loaded` is true, use `playbook.failure_modes` as the primary source. If not, synthesise 2–3 risks from the bear scenario narrative and the sensitivity dominant driver.\n\nFormat as short bullets or short bolded paragraphs — one risk per item. Each risk should name (1) what event happens, and (2) why it breaks the model's specific growth assumption.\n\n**Example (playbook loaded):**\n- **AI capex slowdown:** If cloud providers cut GPU spending — as happened briefly in 2022 — NVIDIA's revenue could plateau and the 35% annual growth the base case assumes would quickly look too optimistic.\n- **Custom-chip displacement:** ASIC chips built by Google, Amazon, and others are already taking share in AI inference. If they exceed 25% of AI workloads by 2027, it removes one of the main tailwinds behind NVIDIA's pricing power.\n- **Export controls:** If the US government tightens rules to cover NVIDIA's next-generation China chips, it eliminates residual China revenue and signals that further restrictions are politically viable.\n\n### E. How much to trust this — with qualitative context\n\nTranslate `meta.confidence` (HIGH / MEDIUM / LOW) to plain language, and *then* go further than the report does. The numerical confidence answers \"how much did the model have to guess?\" but the more useful question for the reader is \"what kind of bet is this, in business terms?\" Add 2–4 sentences of qualitative context drawn from the report's qualitative fields (signal `ai_layer`, `qualitative_note`, scenario narratives, playbook content):\n\n- Where is the company in its market lifecycle? (Early share gains, mature with expansion bets, defending a moat?)\n- What kind of future is the base case implicitly betting on? (Continued AI infrastructure buildout? Successful pivot? Regulatory stability?)\n- What's the most important thing the reader should be watching that the model can't measure?\n\n**Example (NVDA, qualitative context):**\n\n> A company already at roughly 90% share of data-centre AI accelerators has limited room to grow from share gains. Future growth requires the AI compute market itself to keep expanding — a bet on continued hyperscaler capex, sovereign-AI buildouts, and the emergence of new workloads like physical AI. These are real opportunities, but they sit further out and are harder to forecast than the share-gain story of the past three years.\n\nThis kind of context is what separates a useful explanation from a recitation of the model.\n\n---\n\n## 7. Invocation Patterns\n\n```\n/stock-explain NVDA\n/stock-explain META\n/stock-explain AAPL\n/stock-explain MSFT\n```\n\n---\n\n## 8. Dependencies\n\n- `reports/TICKER_YYYYMMDD.json` with both `stages.signal` and `stages.model` populated\n- No MCP tools\n- No external network access\n\n---\n\n## 9. Common Rationalisations\n\n| What you might want to do | Why not to do it |\n|---|---|\n| Fetch live prices to make the output \"current\" | The skill explains the model that was run — live prices would mismatch the report's assumptions. If the report is stale, tell the user to re-run `/stock-signal` and `/stock-model`. |\n| Add technical terms back in for completeness | If a term needs a parenthetical explanation, just use the parenthetical. The goal is comprehension, not precision theatre. |\n| Emit a JSON block for the audit trail | The audit trail is the report JSON file itself. This output is for human reading only. |\n| Skip the FCF/SBC explanation because the user already ran `/stock-model` | The whole point of `/stock-explain` is to build understanding from scratch. Always explain the inputs, regardless of what the user has already run. |\n| Skip the WACC sensitivity / interest rate connection | This is the single most-asked question from non-finance readers. Always quantify a 1pp WACC move and connect it to interest rates. |\n| Skip the implied year-5 FCF calculation | This is the most concrete way to sanity-check the growth assumption. Always compute and state it with a future-year reference. |\n| Reuse the same five literal section headers on every ticker | Headings should fit the company's story. The five *questions* are required; the exact phrasing is not. |\n| Hedge every sentence (\"this may be\", \"it's possible that\") | The model has already handled uncertainty through the bear/base/bull range. Don't pile on additional verbal hedging — it reads as evasive rather than honest. State clearly what the model says, and clearly what the known risks are. |\n"
}

{
  "file_path": "/Users/anton/src/stock-review/skills/stock-explain-workspace/iteration-3/grade.py",
  "content": "\"\"\"Grade iter-3 outputs against assertions defined in evals.json.\"\"\"\nimport json\nimport re\nimport sys\nfrom pathlib import Path\n\nWORKSPACE = Path(\"/Users/anton/src/stock-review/skills/stock-explain-workspace/iteration-3\")\nEVALS = Path(\"/Users/anton/src/stock-review/skills/stock-explain/evals/evals.json\")\n\nEVAL_DIRS = {\n    0: \"eval-0-nvda\",\n    1: \"eval-1-meta\",\n    2: \"eval-2-fake\",\n}\n\n\ndef check_assertion(text: str, assertion: dict) -> tuple[bool, str]:\n    \"\"\"Returns (passed, evidence).\"\"\"\n    check = assertion[\"check\"]\n    if check == \"string_contains_all\":\n        expected = assertion[\"expected\"]\n        missing = [e for e in expected if e.lower() not in text.lower()]\n        if missing:\n            return False, f\"Missing: {missing}\"\n        return True, f\"All present: {expected}\"\n    elif check == \"string_contains_any\":\n        expected = assertion[\"expected\"]\n        found = [e for e in expected if e.lower() in text.lower()]\n        if found:\n            return True, f\"Found: {found[:3]}\"\n        return False, f\"None of {expected} present\"\n    elif check == \"string_not_contains\":\n        expected = assertion[\"expected\"]\n        if expected in text:\n            return False, f\"Forbidden substring present: {expected}\"\n        return True, f\"Forbidden substring absent: {expected}\"\n    elif check == \"regex_match\":\n        pattern = assertion[\"pattern\"]\n        m = re.search(pattern, text, re.DOTALL)\n        if m:\n            snippet = m.group(0)[:200]\n            return True, f\"Matched: {snippet!r}\"\n        return False, f\"No match for pattern\"\n    return False, f\"Unknown check type: {check}\"\n\n\ndef grade_run(eval_id: int, config: str) -> dict:\n    eval_dir = WORKSPACE / EVAL_DIRS[eval_id] / config\n    output_path = eval_dir / \"outputs\" / \"output.txt\"\n    if not output_path.exists():\n        return {\"error\": f\"No output file at {output_path}\"}\n    text = output_path.read_text()\n\n    with EVALS.open() as f:\n        evals = json.load(f)[\"evals\"]\n    eval_def = next(e for e in evals if e[\"id\"] == eval_id)\n\n    results = []\n    for a in eval_def[\"assertions\"]:\n        passed, evidence = check_assertion(text, a)\n        results.append({\n            \"id\": a[\"id\"],\n            \"text\": a[\"description\"],\n            \"passed\": passed,\n            \"evidence\": evidence,\n        })\n\n    grading = {\n        \"eval_id\": eval_id,\n        \"eval_name\": {0: \"nvda-full-report\", 1: \"meta-full-report\", 2: \"fake-ticker-error\"}[eval_id],\n        \"configuration\": config,\n        \"expectations\": results,\n    }\n    grading_path = eval_dir / \"grading.json\"\n    grading_path.write_text(json.dumps(grading, indent=2) + \"\\n\")\n    return grading\n\n\ndef main() -> None:\n    summary = []\n    for eval_id in [0, 1, 2]:\n        for config in [\"with_skill\", \"without_skill\"]:\n            g = grade_run(eval_id, config)\n            passed = sum(1 for e in g[\"expectations\"] if e[\"passed\"])\n            total = len(g[\"expectations\"])\n            summary.append((g[\"eval_name\"], config, passed, total))\n            print(f\"\\n=== {g['eval_name']} / {config}: {passed}/{total} ===\")\n            for e in g[\"expectations\"]:\n                mark = \"PASS\" if e[\"passed\"] else \"FAIL\"\n                print(f\"  [{mark}] {e['id']}: {e['evidence'][:120]}\")\n    print(\"\\n--- SUMMARY ---\")\n    for name, cfg, p, t in summary:\n        print(f\"  {name:24s} {cfg:14s}  {p}/{t}  ({100*p/t:.0f}%)\")\n\n\nif __name__ == \"__main__\":\n    main()\n"
}

```
1	NVIDIA (NVDA) — what the model is saying
2	Report date: 2026-05-20  |  Confidence: MEDIUM  |  Model: two-stage discounted cash flow
3	
4	================================================================
5	The headline
6	================================================================
7	
8	NVIDIA trades at $220.09 today. Our base estimate of what a share is
9	actually worth — its intrinsic value (IV), the model's per-share fair
10	price — is $351.65. That's about 60% above the current price, and the
11	model reads it as a buy.
12	
13	It's worth saying clearly up front: this is not a precise number. The
14	realistic range runs from roughly $140 a share in the bear case to over
15	$700 in the bull case. That spread is the honest signature of a business
16	whose next five years depend on how much the world keeps spending on AI.
17	
18	================================================================
19	How the model gets to $351
20	================================================================
21	
22	The model is a discounted cash flow (DCF) — a method for estimating
23	what a business is worth based on the cash it will generate in the
24	future, then translating that future cash back into today's dollars.
25	Four ingredients go in:
26	
27	1. The starting cash flow.
28	
29	   We start from free cash flow (FCF) — the money the business throws
30	   off after paying to run itself and to invest in growth. NVIDIA's
31	   trailing twelve months (TTM, the most recent full year of data)
32	   produced $96.7 billion in reported FCF. The model uses $90.3 billion
33	   instead, which is the reported number minus $6.4 billion of
34	   stock-based compensation (SBC) — shares issued to employees as pay.
35	   Stock comp dilutes existing shareholders even though it never shows
36	   up as a cash outflow, so we treat it as a real cost. The cleaned-up
37	   FCF is roughly 42% of revenue, which is enormous and tells you most
38	   of what you need to know about the business: it converts a huge
39	   share of every dollar of sales into spendable cash.
40	
41	2. The growth rate for the next five years.
42	
43	   This is where most of the disagreement in valuing NVIDIA lives. The
44	   model uses 35% annual FCF growth for years two through five in the
45	   base case. That came from the ticker-specific playbook the model
46	   loaded for NVDA — not from analyst consensus and not from the raw
47	   historical trend. Why a cap was applied: the trailing three-year
48	   FCF CAGR (compound annual growth rate) is technically 335% because
49	   the 2022→2025 ramp was so extreme, but extrapolating that forward
50	   is nonsense. The playbook ceiling of 35% is meant to reflect what
51	   is actually defensible over a five-year horizon given NVIDIA's size.
52	
53	3. The discount rate.
54	
55	   The weighted-average cost of capital (WACC) is, in plain terms, the
56	   annual return investors require to take on the risk of owning this
57	   stock. The base case uses 9%, also from the NVDA playbook. A lower
58	   WACC means future cash flows are worth more today; a higher WACC
59	   means they're worth less.
60	
61	4. The mechanics.
62	
63	   We project five years of free cash flow at the chosen growth rate,
64	   add a terminal value (an estimate of what the business is worth
65	   from year six onward at a slower, steady-state growth rate of 2.5%),
66	   and discount the whole stream back to today's dollars at the WACC.
67	   Divide by ~24.5 billion diluted shares, and you get $351.65.
68	
69	================================================================
70	What's actually driving the number
71	================================================================
72	
73	Two things move the answer more than anything else, and you should
74	look at them directly.
75	
76	The discount rate, and what that means about interest rates.
77	
78	The model itself flags WACC as the dominant driver: a 1 percentage point
79	change moves the per-share value by roughly 17%. Dropping the discount
80	rate from 9% to 8% would push the base estimate from $351.65 to about
81	$423. Raising it from 9% to 10% drags it down to about $300.
82	
83	What does this have to do with you, the reader? WACC moves with
84	long-term interest rates. When the 10-year Treasury yield rises, the
85	required return on stocks rises with it, and our IV compresses. If you
86	believe long rates have a lot further to climb, the NVDA fair value
87	narrows toward (or below) the current price even without anything
88	changing inside NVIDIA. If you believe rates are headed lower over the
89	next few years, the same model gives a more generous estimate.
90	
91	The 35% growth rate, made concrete.
92	
93	Compounding 35% annually for five years is the kind of number that
94	sounds abstract on the page, so it's worth turning it into a real
95	quantity. Starting from $208 billion of free cash flow in year one,
96	35% growth through year five lands at roughly $691 billion of annual
97	free cash flow by 2031 — about 7.7 times what the business generates
98	today.
99	
100	Is that achievable? It's the right question to ask. For context, that
101	would put NVIDIA's annual cash generation comfortably above the entire
102	current revenue of any company on Earth except a handful of state oil
103	producers. The model isn't saying this is guaranteed; it is saying that
104	*if* it happens, $351 a share is the fair value. The bear scenario
105	($140) is what you get if growth comes in much slower — 17.5% a year
106	instead of 35% — and the bull ($712) is what you get if growth holds
107	above 40% and the AI compute market keeps expanding into new domains.
108	
109	The per-scenario stories the model is telling:
110	
111	  - Base ($351, +60% upside): hyperscaler AI capex stays heavy through
112	    2027 on the Blackwell upgrade cycle; the CUDA software ecosystem
113	    keeps NVIDIA at 65–70% data-centre GPU share; sovereign-AI buildouts
114	    and enterprise rollout cushion any slowdown from US hyperscalers;
115	    FCF margin holds near today's 40–42%.
116	
117	  - Bear ($140, -37% downside): custom AI chips designed by Broadcom
118	    and Marvell take meaningful share in inference workloads;
119	    hyperscaler capex decelerates as AI return on investment
120	    disappoints; export controls tighten to cover H20-successor China
121	    chips; AMD and Intel finally land credible alternatives. Revenue
122	    and margin compress at the same time.
123	
124	  - Bull ($712, +224% upside): physical AI — robotics and autonomous
125	    vehicles — opens a second total addressable market (TAM) on top
126	    of the data centre; sovereign AI buildouts in Europe, the Middle
127	    East, and Asia provide demand independent of US cloud-spending
128	    cycles; CUDA becomes harder to dislodge as models get more complex;
129	    export controls don't get worse.
130	
131	================================================================
132	What could break the base case
133	================================================================
134	
135	The playbook lays out the specific failure modes that would invalidate
136	the 35%-growth assumption. These are the events to watch for:
137	
138	  - Hyperscaler capex deceleration. Roughly 70% of NVIDIA's data-centre
139	    revenue concentrates in the big four (Microsoft, Google, Amazon,
140	    Meta). If any two of them announce AI infrastructure cuts in the
141	    same quarter, the revenue base under the 35% assumption goes away
142	    quickly.
143	
144	  - Custom-silicon penetration. If application-specific chips (ASICs)
145	    designed by Broadcom and Marvell exceed about 25% of AI inference
146	    workloads by 2027, the inference-scaling tailwind that supports
147	    CUDA's lock-in thesis is gone.
148	
149	  - Export-control escalation. If the US extends restrictions to cover
150	    H20-successor chips — or gaming-grade chips being repurposed for
151	    inference in China — residual China revenue disappears and the
152	    political direction signals more restrictions to come.
153	
154	  - CUDA ecosystem erosion. If PyTorch or JAX achieve hardware-neutral
155	    performance parity at scale, the switching cost that keeps AI
156	    workloads on NVIDIA silicon weakens.
157	
158	  - Blackwell-successor stumble. A defect or delay greater than two
159	    quarters on the next architecture would break the cadence of
160	    upgrades that hyperscalers commit to in advance — and pricing power
161	    depends on that cadence.
162	
163	  - TAM ceiling. The signal phase flagged this directly: data-centre
164	    GPU total addressable market estimates land around $300–350
165	    billion, and NVIDIA's annualised DC revenue is already around $188
166	    billion — roughly 63% penetration. The base case implicitly assumes
167	    the TAM expands (physical AI, edge inference) rather than simply
168	    being saturated.
169	
170	================================================================
171	How much to trust this
172	================================================================
173	
174	The model's confidence is MEDIUM. That means the inputs are reasonable
175	but the bet has real uncertainty — most of which is sitting in the
176	growth assumption and the discount rate. The numerical confidence does
177	not capture the full story, though, so:
178	
179	NVIDIA already controls something like 90% of the data-centre AI
180	accelerator market. That means almost none of the next leg of growth
181	can come from taking share — there isn't much share left to take.
182	Future growth requires the AI compute market itself to keep getting
183	bigger: continued hyperscaler capex, sovereign-AI infrastructure
184	buildouts, the emergence of new workloads like physical AI, and edge
185	inference. These are real opportunities, but they sit further out and
186	are harder to forecast than the share-gain story of 2023–2025.
187	
188	The most important thing to watch — the thing the model can't measure
189	directly — is the rate of AI-compute demand growth from the largest
190	spenders. Specifically: do the big four hyperscalers continue to commit
191	capex at the levels their 2025 guidance implied, and does sovereign-AI
192	spending start to materialise as a meaningful demand source? If both
193	of those hold, the base case is in reach. If hyperscaler capex
194	plateaus, the model compresses toward the bear scenario regardless of
195	how impressive the technology stays.
196	
197	Report date: 2026-05-20. The numbers above reflect the data the model
198	was run on; if more than a few weeks have passed since then, re-run
199	/stock-signal and /stock-model before leaning on these figures.
200

1	META — Plain-English walkthrough
2	Report date: 2026-05-19  |  Confidence: HIGH
3	
4	The headline
5	------------
6	Meta is trading at about $611 a share. Our model thinks it's actually worth around $811 in the base case — roughly 33% above today's price. The signal is WATCH rather than an outright "buy": the gap is real, but the model wants to see one or two things resolve (mostly around Reality Labs spending and EU/US antitrust) before pounding the table. The full plausible range from bear to bull is wide — $398 on the downside, $1,523 on the upside — and that spread is the more honest answer than the single base number.
7	
8	How we built the $811
9	---------------------
10	The model is a discounted cash flow (DCF) — that's a method that estimates what a business is worth based on the cash it will throw off in the future, after translating those future dollars back into today's dollars. Four inputs do most of the work:
11	
12	1. Starting cash flow. We use $25.7 billion of trailing twelve-month (TTM, i.e. the most recent full year of) free cash flow (FCF — the money left after the business pays to run and grow itself). The reported figure is much higher at $46.1 billion, but we strip out $20.4 billion of stock-based compensation (SBC — shares granted to employees as pay). SBC doesn't leave the bank account, but it dilutes existing shareholders, so for valuation purposes it's a real cost. The cleaned-up number is the honest starting point.
13	
14	2. Growth assumption. We grow that cash flow at 18% a year for five years in the base case. Meta's actual three-year FCF growth rate has been roughly 52% — that's the post-2022 "Year of Efficiency" rebound — and using it raw would be misleading because it's a recovery off a depressed 2022 base, not a sustainable forward rate. The META playbook caps growth at 18%, which is closer to where mid-teens revenue growth plus modest margin expansion lands.
15	
16	3. Discount rate. We use a 9.0% weighted-average cost of capital (WACC — roughly, the annual return investors require to take on the risk of owning this stock). This comes from the META playbook rather than a generic default. It reflects Meta's mature cash generation but also its regulatory and capex risk.
17	
18	4. The mechanics. We project five years of free cash flow at the growth rate above, add a terminal value (an estimate of what the business is worth from year six onward at a slower, steady-state 2.5% growth), and discount everything back to today's dollars at the 9% WACC. Divide by 2.57 billion diluted shares, adjust for $48 billion of net debt, and you get the per-share intrinsic value (IV — the model's estimate of what a share is actually worth).
19	
20	What's actually moving the number
21	---------------------------------
22	Two things deserve the spotlight here.
23	
24	The discount rate is the single biggest lever. The report flags WACC as the dominant driver: a 100 basis point (1 percentage point) change in WACC moves the base IV by about ±17%. Concretely, raising WACC from 9% to 10% pulls the base estimate down from $811 to roughly $694; cutting it from 9% to 8% pushes it up to about $884. The macro read across is straightforward — if long-term interest rates rise, WACC rises with them, and the model's estimate compresses accordingly. If rates fall, the opposite. A lot of what looks like "Meta-specific" valuation movement on any given day is really just the bond market reshuffling underneath.
25	
26	The growth assumption is the other big lever, and it's worth making it concrete. Growing $84 billion of year-one free cash flow at 18% a year for four more years implies that by 2031 Meta would be generating roughly $164 billion of free cash flow annually. That's about 6.4× today's SBC-cleaned $25.7 billion — or 3.5× the reported $46 billion. For context, that puts year-5 Meta in the rough neighbourhood of what the entire S&P 500's cash-cow tech cohort generates today. Whether you find that plausible is the real question the base case is asking. The bull case ($1,523) implies even more — closer to $200 billion at year five. The bear case ($398) implies Meta's free cash flow is structurally capped much closer to where it is now.
27	
28	What has to be true in each scenario
29	- Base ($811): The Family of Apps (Facebook, Instagram, WhatsApp, Threads) keeps growing revenue in the mid-teens on the back of better AI ad-targeting. Reality Labs losses stabilise below $20 billion a year and smart-glasses hardware finds enough commercial traction to argue it's a real product line, not a money pit. Regulatory fines stay manageable — no forced break-up.
30	- Bull ($1,523): The same story plus a step-change. AI-driven ad-targeting unlocks materially better return on ad spend for advertisers, re-accelerating revenue above consensus. Smart glasses pivot toward profitable hardware. EU/US regulators close out their cases without forcing structural separation, and the regulatory overhang re-rates as a non-event.
31	- Bear ($398): Reality Labs cash drain intensifies rather than stabilising. Antitrust action lands hard — either EU structural remedy or US action against Instagram/WhatsApp — and forces business separation. AI ad-targeting fails to meaningfully improve ROAS, especially through a macro downturn. Family of Apps revenue decelerates while capex stays elevated.
32	
33	The bear-base-bull spread is wide ($398 to $1,523) because two big things — the growth rate and the WACC — both have genuine uncertainty around them. That's not a flaw in the model; it's an honest report of where the disagreement lives.
34	
35	What could break this
36	---------------------
37	The META playbook lays these out specifically:
38	
39	- DAP (Daily Active People) growth decelerates below 3% year-over-year — the engagement floor that underpins everything else cracks, and the monetisation-leverage story falls apart.
40	- EU antitrust forces structural separation of Messenger, WhatsApp, or Instagram from the core Facebook platform — a structural remedy, not a fine.
41	- US DOJ action seeking dissolution of the Instagram and/or WhatsApp acquisitions succeeds.
42	- Reality Labs capex stays sustainably above $20 billion a year without a credible near-term path to monetisation. The free cash flow drag becomes thesis-breaking — that's a real risk because Reality Labs is already the biggest gap between reported FCF and our cleaned-up FCF.
43	- AI-generated content floods the feed and degrades ad-targeting signal quality. This would look a lot like Apple's privacy-sandbox shock in 2021, but self-inflicted — ROAS deterioration without a regulatory catalyst.
44	- Threads fails to retain users at scale and never produces meaningful ad revenue by 2027. This wouldn't break the base case on its own, but it eliminates the third-pillar optionality embedded in it.
45	
46	How much to trust this
47	----------------------
48	The model is HIGH confidence — meaning the inputs are well-sourced, the playbook is loaded with Meta-specific assumptions, and there's no big "we had to guess" footnote. The signal output also flagged that Meta's AI investment looks proportionate to the core business (the AI optionality premium is only about 15% of the value), so we're not paying a wild AI-hype tax on top.
49	
50	That said, the numerical confidence isn't the whole story. Meta is a mature business with two genuinely uncertain narratives stapled to it. The first is regulatory: both the EU and the US have open cases that could, in their most aggressive form, force structural separation of Meta's main assets. The base case quietly assumes that doesn't happen. The second is Reality Labs: the company is spending heavily on a hardware and AR bet that may or may not pay off in the 5–10 year window. The base case assumes losses stabilise rather than escalate.
51	
52	The thing the model can't measure is whether Mark Zuckerberg's long-horizon spending discipline holds. If Reality Labs capex stays elevated indefinitely while the Family of Apps quietly does the work, today's price might already be the right answer — that's basically the bear case. If the Family of Apps keeps compounding and Reality Labs hits or just lands gracefully, $811 will look conservative in hindsight.
53	
54	That's why the verdict is WATCH, not BUY. The math works, but the bet you're making is on regulatory outcomes and capital allocation discipline — both of which will tip their hand over the next 12–18 months.
55	
```

passed,\n            \"total\": total,\n            \"time_seconds\": round(timing[\"duration_ms\"] / 1000, 1),\n            \"tokens\": timing[\"total_tokens\"],\n            \"tool_calls\": 0,\n            \"errors\": 0,\n        },\n        \"expectations\": grading[\"expectations\"],\n        \"notes\": [],\n    }\n\n\ndef main():\n    runs = []\n    # Interleave: with_skill then without_skill for each eval\n    for eval_id, eval_name, eval_dir in EVALS:\n        runs.append(load_run(eval_id, eval_name, eval_dir, \"with_skill\"))\n        runs.append(load_run(eval_id, eval_name, eval_dir, \"without_skill\"))\n\n    def stats(values):\n        return {\n            \"mean\": round(statistics.mean(values), 3) if values else 0,\n            \"stddev\": round(statistics.stdev(values), 3) if len(values) > 1 else 0.0,\n            \"min\": round(min(values), 3) if values else 0,\n            \"max\": round(max(values), 3) if values else 0,\n        }\n\n    def summarise(cfg):\n        rs = [r for r in runs if r[\"configuration\"] == cfg]\n        return {\n            \"pass_rate\": stats([r[\"result\"][\"pass_rate\"] for r in rs]),\n            \"time_seconds\": stats([r[\"result\"][\"time_seconds\"] for r in rs]),\n            \"tokens\": stats([r[\"result\"][\"tokens\"] for r in rs]),\n        }\n\n    with_skill = summarise(\"with_skill\")\n    without_skill = summarise(\"without_skill\")\n    delta = {\n        \"pass_rate\": f\"{with_skill['pass_rate']['mean'] - 1	# Roadmap
2	
3	Last updated: 2026-05-17
4	Cycle: 2026 H1 reshape
5	
6	This roadmap was reshaped after the operator's M5-era learnings about how the pack is actually being used (portfolio-level decisions, opportunity-cost vs benchmarks, triangulation with external tools, plain-language interpretability). It replaces any prior implicit prioritisation.
7	
8	## Strategic priorities
9	
10	1. **Make existing IVs trustworthy.** Without correctness fixes landed, no output is decision-grade. P0.
11	2. **Deepen coverage on the seven holdings.** Playbooks are how depth-over-breadth becomes real. The covered list = the actual holdings; per-ticker depth is the differentiator.
12	3. **Make outputs legible.** Plain-language narrative + trajectory visuals + "explain this number" so the operator understands and trusts each output.
13	4. **Portfolio + opportunity-cost view.** See the seven as a set; compare IV-vs-price gaps and forward returns against SPY / QQQ baselines.
14	5. **External triangulation discipline.** Codify the AlphaSpread reconciliation pattern as a checklist (no build) so deltas with third-party IVs get reasoned about, not ignored.
15	
16	## Cycle 1 goal (2026-05-17 → 2026-05-24)
17	
18	Turn `/stock-model` from a generic intrinsic value calculator into a reliable stock analysis tool that can be used across the portfolio. Fix the remaining trust issues, make the reasoning behind each valuation easy to understand, show deeper company-specific analysis on the first two stocks, and support portfolio-wide comparisons in a single run.
19	
20	### Outcomes — how we'll know it worked
21	
22	1. **The model can be trusted.** The remaining free cash flow base-year issue (ABA-104) is either fixed or clearly documented with evidence and a recommended next step. The model output no longer carries major reliability warnings.
23	2. **The analysis is tailored to each company.** At least two of the seven covered stocks use company-specific assumptions instead of generic defaults. The output clearly shows which assumptions were changed and why.
24	3. **Portfolio comparisons work in one step.** The model can answer questions like "Which stock currently has the biggest gap between intrinsic value and market price?" without manual work outside the tool.
25	4. **The valuation is understandable to non-experts.** A reader can follow how a valuation was produced, what assumptions matter most, and what could change the result, while still being able to trace the underlying data.
26	5. **The model shows how outcomes can change.** At least one stock model includes a visual view of bear, base, and bull scenarios, along with how the valuation changes over time, directly in the UI.
27	
28	### Bets backing each outcome
29	
30	| Outcome | Backing issue(s) |
31	|---|---|
32	| 1 — Trusted model | ABA-104 (base-year FCF spike) |
33	| 2 — Company-specific analysis | ABA-112 (playbook loader) + ABA-116 (ASML) + ABA-117 (GOOG) |
34	| 3 — Portfolio comparisons | ABA-119 (`/stock-portfolio`) |
35	| 4 — Understandable valuations | ABA-118 (`/stock-explain`) |
36	| 5 — Scenario / trajectory visuals | ABA-115 (glidepath + scenario fan) |
37	
38	### Anti-goals (deliberately not this cycle)
39	
40	- New ticker coverage beyond the seven
41	- OSS release work
42	- UI polish beyond the Model tab (Signal / Timing / Summary / badges / PDF)
43	- M6 router orchestration
44	- M2.5 MCP data gaps (ABA-67 / 68 / 69 / 75)
45	
46	### Cycle health check at 2026-05-24
47	
48	Per roadmap Rule D1 + universal Rule D2 (post-launch impact review):
49	
50	- For each shipped bet: did it produce the outcome it was meant to?
51	- For the playbooks: did the overrides change the IV materially, or only cosmetically — and by how much?
52	- Did `/stock-explain` and `/stock-portfolio` get used after they shipped, or sit idle?
53	- What's the smallest next bet to make each outcome stick?
54	
55	## Capacity allocation
56	
57	| Category | % of capacity |
58	|---|---|
59	| Tech foundation (correctness) | 25% |
60	| Differentiator — coverage depth (playbooks) | 30% |
61	| Differentiator — legibility (narrative + visuals) | 20% |
62	| Differentiator — portfolio + benchmark | 15% |
63	| Table-stakes — router + data gaps | 10% |
64	| Embarrassments / KTLO / Speculative | 0% (deliberate; absorbed into Later) |
65	| **Total** | **100%** |
66	
67	Customer specials: N/A — single-operator pack. The mix above is intentionally heavy on differentiators because the pack's whole reason for existing is depth on a curated set.
68

100	
101	## Now
102	
103	### ABA-110 SBC strip + ABA-111 growth-rate ceiling — DONE 2026-05-17
104	**Problem:** For the operator, un-stripped SBC and trough-extrapolated FCF growth were causing `/stock-model` IVs to be systematically inflated — making every output non-decision-grade.
105	**Outcome:** Both shipped same day. `/stock-model` now strips SBC from the FCF base and applies a consensus/fallback growth-rate ceiling. Reports going forward are decision-grade on the correctness axis; CLAUDE.md "built on sand" caveat lifts.
106	**Theme:** tech foundation
107	**Follow-up:** ABA-104 spike (base-year FCF effect) still open — paper-cover that ABA-111 only partially addresses.
108	
109	### ABA-104 Base-year FCF growth-rate spike
110	**Problem:** For the operator, we believe the choice of FCF base-year is causing unstable growth-rate inputs (one weak quarter dominates the projection). Without resolving this, ABA-111 partially papers over the symptom rather than fixing the cause.
111	**Success criterion:** Spike output recommends a base-year selection rule (rolling avg vs trailing single year vs analyst NTM); recommendation either implemented or filed as a follow-up issue with rationale.
112	**Appetite:** 2 days. Spike, not a build.
113	**Theme:** tech foundation
114	
115	### ABA-112 Playbook loader for watchlist tickers
116	**Problem:** For the operator, we believe generic ESTABLISHED/EMERGING defaults are causing per-ticker output to smooth over the specialist knowledge (capex-cycle position, segment mix, governance facts) that's the whole reason the seven names are covered.
117	**Success criterion:** `/stock-model TICKER` on a covered ticker loads `playbooks/TICKER.md`, applies its overrides, and names which fields were overridden in the OUTPUT block. Verified on at least one playbook end-to-end.
118	**Appetite:** ~1 week. Cap.
119	**Theme:** differentiator
120	
121	### Write playbook: ASML
122	**Problem:** For the operator, we believe ASML's EUV-monopoly + capex-cycle + China-overhang structure cannot be captured by generic ESTABLISHED defaults. Without a playbook, the IV will look reasonable and be wrong on the swing factor.
123	**Success criterion:** `playbooks/ASML.md` written per the structure in COVERAGE.md; `/stock-model ASML` produces output that reflects the playbook overrides (cap source named, narrative applied, audit trail intact).
124	**Appetite:** 2–3 days after ABA-112 lands.
125	**Theme:** differentiator
126	
127	### Write playbook: GOOG
128	**Problem:** As above, for GOOG — the AI-search-disruption debate is the central question and generic defaults won't engage with it.
129	**Success criterion:** `playbooks/GOOG.md` written; `/stock-model GOOG` reflects overrides; sell-side disagreement axes are surfaced in output.
130	**Appetite:** 2–3 days after ABA-112 lands.
131	**Theme:** differentiator
132	
133	### `/stock-explain` skill
134	**Problem:** For the operator, we believe model outputs use sophisticated terminology that's correct but not understandable on a quick read — making the IV a "stupid number" rather than something usable. Existing skills' methodology sections are present but dense.
135	**Success criterion:** New `/stock-explain TICKER` skill takes the latest report for a ticker and produces a plain-English walkthrough — what the number means, how it was derived, what assumptions are doing the work, what would change it. Tested on two reports; operator confirms it reads as friendly without losing the audit trail.
136	**Appetite:** ~1 week.
137	**Theme:** differentiator (legibility)
138	**Notes:** Separate skill, not an inline output section — keeps existing outputs unchanged and lets the explainer evolve independently. Per operator: "i need to understand how the valuation system works."
139	
140	### `/stock-portfolio` skill
141	**Problem:** For the operator, we believe the current per-ticker pattern is causing portfolio-level questions ("which of my seven is most overvalued right now?") to require manual aggregation — which means they don't happen.
142	**Success criterion:** `/stock-portfolio` runs the screen + signal (and reads latest cached model) on all seven covered tickers, produces a ranked table: ticker / current price / our IV / margin of safety / verdict / last updated. Operator can answer "where should I add" in one invocation.
143	**Appetite:** ~1 week. v1 reads from `reports/`; does not re-run model every time.
144	**Theme:** differentiator (portfolio)
145	
146	### ABA-115 Glidepath + scenario fan visuals
147	**Problem:** For the operator, we believe text-only model outputs are causing the future trajectory of a company to be hard to internalise. Visuals would shorten the path from "I read the IV" to "I see what it implies."
148	**Success criterion:** `/stock-model` report includes a glidepath visual (revenue/FCF projection) and scenario fan (bear/base/bull); rendered in UI Model tab.
149	**Appetite:** ~1 week.
150	**Theme:** differentiator (legibility)
151	
152	## Next
153	
154	### Write remaining playbooks: META, NVDA, AMZN, NFLX, ADYEN
155	**Problem:** Five of the seven covered tickers still run on generic defaults. The whole depth-over-breadth thesis is unrealised until all seven have playbooks.
156	**Theme:** differentiator
157	
158	### Benchmark overlay (SPY / QQQ comparator)
159	**Problem:** For the operator, we believe per-ticker IVs don't answer the actual question — "should I buy this or just buy the index?" Without a benchmark layer, the pack can't help with opportunity-cost decisions, which is the second-most-common reason it's used.
160	**Theme:** differentiator (portfolio)
161	**Notes:** Likely lives as an addition to `/stock-portfolio` rather than a separate skill — column for "implied annualised return vs QQQ over [horizon]."
162	
163	### M2.5 data gaps — ABA-67, ABA-68, ABA-69, ABA-75
164	**Problem:** Bookings/backlog (capital-equipment cos), EPS revisions, next-earnings-date, and EDGAR SBC fallback (for non-US filers like ADYEN) are missing. `/stock-timing` and `/stock-model` quality is capped until these land.
165	**Theme:** table-stakes
166	
167	### ABA-65 Segment-revenue trend as growth modifier
168	**Problem:** For tickers where blended growth hides segment divergence (AWS in AMZN, Cloud in GOOG, DC in NVDA), blended NTM estimates produce wrong growth inputs.
169	**Theme:** differentiator
170	
171	### M6 Router — ABA-36/37/38/39/62/63
172	**Problem:** Manual chaining of Screen → Signal → Model works but creates friction; the router is how the pack becomes a daily tool rather than a per-decision tool.
173	**Theme:** table-stakes
174	**Notes:** Kept per operator decision — chain orchestration is core UX even though it's six issues.
175	
176	### UI Model tab + sensitivity table — ABA-42, ABA-43
177	**Problem:** Model outputs need a structured place to live; sensitivity is best read interactively.
178	**Theme:** differentiator (legibility)
179	
180	## Later
181	
182	### AlphaSpread reconciliation checklist (doc, not build)
183	**Hypothesis:** A written checklist for reconciling our IV against AlphaSpread's IV — where to look for the largest deltas (WACC, terminal growth, FCF base, capex schedule), how to decide which is right, when to update our model vs note an unreconciled disagreement — would capture most of the value of a build, with none of the brittleness.
184	**Theme:** differentiator
185	**Notes:** Per operator: ship as `docs/operations/reconciliation-checklist.md` when it's the next-most-valuable thing. No build slot.
186	
187	### UI polish — ABA-40, ABA-41, ABA-44, ABA-45, ABA-46
188	**Hypothesis:** Once Model tab + visuals are landed, the Signal / Timing / Summary tabs, verdict badges, and PDF export round out the UI. Lower urgency than the differentiator work above.
189	**Theme:** incremental
190	
191	### ABA-64 Playwright e2e suite
192	**Hypothesis:** Worth doing once the UI surface is stable — running it earlier means rewriting tests every cycle.
193	**Theme:** tech foundation
194	
195	### ABA-107 / ABA-108 KPI coverage page + ticker-add helper
196	**Hypothesis:** Useful as the pack matures, low marginal value while only seven tickers are covered.
197	**Theme:** incremental
198	
199	### Spikes / ADR / drift live / parity — ABA-105, ABA-103, ABA-109, ABA-113, ABA-95
200	**Hypothesis:** Quality-of-life and structural cleanups. Important, not urgent.
201	**Theme:** tech foundation
202	
203	### M8 OSS release — ABA-50 / 51 / 52 / 53 / 54 / 55 / 56 / 57
204	**Hypothesis:** Still want to ship publicly, but personal-tool usability comes first. Kept per operator decision; revisit after Now and Next clear.
205	**Theme:** speculative
206	
207	## Validation slots
208	
209	None this cycle. All Now items are confidence ≥ 6; the build-vs-validation gate doesn't trigger. `/stock-portfolio` is the lowest-confidence Now item (6) and is a small build, so the appropriate move is to ship v1 and learn from usage rather than spike it separately.
210

213	| Pattern | Status | Notes |
214	|---|---|---|
215	| Customer-specials dominance | ok | N/A — single-operator pack |
216	| Tech foundation starvation | ok | 25% allocated; correctness fixes are P0 |
217	| Differentiators at low confidence | ok | All `diff` items in Now are confidence ≥ 6; ABA-112 + playbooks at 7–8 |
218	| No speculative bets | flagged | Speculative is 0% this cycle. Intentional — pack is in "make trustworthy + deepen coverage" mode, not exploration mode. OSS release sits in Later as the speculative slot when it reactivates. |
219	| Must-be gaps unfixed | ok | ABA-110/111 (the must-be gap) are in flight in Now |
220	| Validation items in build slots | ok | No low-confidence items in build slots |
221	
222	## What's deliberately absent this cycle
223	
224	- **OSS release work** — Later, per operator. Personal-tool quality first.
225	- **New ticker coverage beyond the seven** — depth-over-breadth principle stands. Adding a name is a deliberate contribution, not a casual edit.
226	- **Non-tech instrument support (GLDM etc.)** — out of scope per CHARTER.
227	- **AlphaSpread scraping / MCP** — doc-only reconciliation chosen over a brittle build.
